Categorical Realizability for Non-symmetric Closed Structures
Haruka Tomita

Abstract
. In categorical realizability, it is common to construct categories of assemblies and categories of modest sets from applicative structures. These categories have structures corresponding to the structures of applicative structures. In the literature, classes of applicative structures inducing categorical structures such as Cartesian closed categories and symmetric monoidal closed categories have been widely studied. In this paper, we expand these correspondences between categories with structure and applicative structures by identifying the classes of applicative structures giving rise to closed multicategories, closed categories, monoidal bi-closed categories as well as (non-symmetric) monoidal closed categories. These applicative structures are planar in that they correspond to appropriate planar lambda calculi by combinatory completeness. These new correspondences are tight: we show that, when a category of assemblies has one of the structures listed above, the based applicative structure is in the corresponding class. In addition, we introduce planar linear combinatory algebras by adopting linear combinatory algebras of Abramsky, Hagjverdi and Scott to our planar setting, that give rise to categorical models of the linear exponential modality and the exchange modality on the non-symmetric multiplicative intuitionistic linear logic.



1.
Introduction

Realizability
started with
[
Kle45
]
to give interpretations for Heyting arithmetic, and subsequently has been developed in many directions.
The
categorical realizability
we call here is one such development, giving categorical models of various programming languages and logics.
Given a very simple algebraic structure
{\mathcal{A}}
called
applicative structure
(or often called
combinatory algebra
), we construct categories
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
used as categorical models.
For an applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}
, the category of assemblies
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is the category of “
{\mathcal{A}}
-computable universe” and its categorical structure depends on the computational structure of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Therefore, giving
{\mathcal{A}}
certain conditions, we obtain
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
with corresponding categorical structures.

The best known is that the condition of
{\mathcal{A}}
being a
partial combinatory algebra (PCA)
leads that
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) is a Cartesian closed category (CCC)
[
Lon95
]
.
A PCA is an applicative structure containing two special elements
{\bf S}
and
{\bf K}
which expresses substitution and discarding.
(We often call
{\bf S}
-combinator or
{\bf K}
-combinator as such elements.)
PCAs also can be characterized by the
combinatory completeness
, that is, the property that any computable functions (i.e., functions expressed as untyped lambda terms) on a PCA can be represented by elements of the PCA itself.

Categorical realizability for linear structures is also well investigated.
Assuming
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BCI}
-
algebra
, that have combinators
{\bf B}
,
{\bf C}
and
{\bf I}
,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
become symmetric monoidal closed categories (SMCCs)
[
AL05
]
.

{\bf B}
,
{\bf C}
and
{\bf I}
are combinators expressing composition, exchanging and identity operations respectively, and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras correspond to the linear lambda calculus by the combinatory completeness.
These results for PCAs and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras are used as an useful method to giving various models based on CCCs and SMCCs.

On the other hand, categorical realizability based on non-symmetric structures has been less investigated.
In our previous studies
[
Tom21
,
Tom22
]
, we proposed “planar realizability” giving rise to non-symmetric categorical structures, such as closed multicategories, closed categories, skew closed categories and monoidal bi-closed categories.
The aim of this paper is to summarize and develop these results.

First in section
2
, we start with recalling basic notions of categorical realizability.
Results of PCAs and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras are shown in the section.
Also notions of
applicative morphisms
and
linear combinatory algebras (LCAs)
are recalled from
[
Lon95
,
AHS02
,
Hos07
]
, that are used to obtain models of linear exponential modalities on linear calculus.
Basic knowledge of category theory and the lambda calculus is assumed and not referred here.

Next in section
3
, we introduce several classes of applicative structures inducing non-symmetric categorical structures.
Realizing non-symmetric closed structures is a more subtle problem than the symmetric cases like CCCs and SMCCs.
Since the
{\bf C}
-combinator in
{\bf BCI}
-algebras induces the symmetry of the monoidal structure on the category of assemblies, one may think we can obtain non-symmetric categorical structures by excluding the
{\bf C}
-combinator.
However, simply excluding the
{\bf C}
-combinator leads no interesting categorical structures like internal hom functors, since realizing closed structures needs some exchanging of realizers even if the closed structures are not symmetric.
We have to give applicative structures with appropriately weakened exchanging that realizes internal hom structures but does not realize symmetries.
To resolve this problem, in
[
Tom21
]
, we introduced a unary operation
{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
on an applicative structure, which allows restricted exchanging.
In section
3.1
and
3.2
, we recall these results, that
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-
algebras
induce (non-symmetric) closed multicategories and
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-
algebras
induce closed categories.
By the combinatory completeness, these classes of applicative structures correspond to the
planar lambda calculus
.

By the unary operation
{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
, we obtain non-symmetric closed structures, however, this operation is not sufficient to obtain non-symmetric
monoidal
structures.
Assume that
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
on a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
has tensor products.
When we take realizers of tensor products of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
in the same way that we take realizers of Cartesian/tensor products of assemblies on PCAs/
{\bf BCI}
-algebras, the realizer of unitors of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
leads a realizer of the symmetry.
That is, this attempt to get non-symmetric tensor products from
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras ends in failure that the tensor products are symmetric.
Here what matters is that the way realizing products of assemblies on PCAs/
{\bf BCI}
-algebras corresponds to the representation of tensor products

X\otimes Y\cong\forall\alpha.(X\multimap Y\multimap\alpha)\multimap\alpha

in the second-order linear logic (
cf.

[
BMP06
]
), which is valid only if the tensor is symmetric.
Thus, categorical realizability for non-symmetric monoidal structures needs some modification on the way realizing tensor products.

In this paper, we give two answers for this problem.
One is the way preparing a new combinator
{\bf P}
which directly realizes pairings.
The class of applicative structures,
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-
algebras
, is newly introduced in this paper and give rise to non-symmetric monoidal closed categories.
We show results about
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras in section
3.3
.
The other way is taking realizers of tensor products matching the representation

X\otimes Y\cong\forall\alpha.(\alpha\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\multimap\alpha

in the second-order linear logic, which is valid even in the non-symmetric case.
To give such realizers, the class of applicative structures,
bi
-
{\bf BDI}
-
algebras
, was introduced in
[
Tom22
]
.
Bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras feature two kinds of applications corresponding to two kinds of implications

\multimap

and
\multimap
, and have the combinatory completeness for the lambda calculus with two kinds of applications (which we call the
bi-planar lambda calculus
in this paper).
In section
3.4
, we recall these results about bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras.

Classes of applicative structures appearing in this paper are summarized in Table
1
.
Also combinators and operations are summarized in Table
2
.

{(-)}^{\bullet}

unary operation
{(-)}^{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}\triangleleft}}

({x}^{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}\triangleleft}})\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y=y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}x

unary operation
{(-)}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}

y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}({x}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})=x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y

The classes of applicative structures in this paper form a hierarchy as summarized in Table
1
.
In section
4
, we show that these classes are different from each other.
To show the strictness of the inclusion, it is sufficient to give examples belonging to one side and not to the other side, and we give such examples in section
4.1
.
While these proofs in section
4.1
are mostly straightforward and not conceptually new, sometimes it is not easy to show that some applicative structure does not belong to some class of applicative structures.
As such an example, in section
4.2
, we show that the untyped planar lambda calculus (with no constants) is not a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.
In the next section
4.3
, we give the
computational lambda calculus

[
Mog88
]
as a rather unexpected example of a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra and show the computational lambda calculus is not a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.

To better clarify the relationship between applicative structures and categorical structures of categories of assemblies, in section
5
, we show certain “inverses” of propositions shown in section
3
.
That is, assuming
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
has certain categorical structure (such as being an SMCC), we show
{\mathcal{A}}
belongs to the corresponding class (such as
{\bf BCI}
-algebras) under several conditions.
While the propositions for the cases of
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras were already presented in
[
Tom21
]
, those for the cases of
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras are newly shown in this paper.
By integrating results of section
3
,
4
and
5
, we can say that, for instance, the category of assemblies on the planar lambda calculus indeed has non-symmetric closed structure.

In section
6
, we reformulate notions of LCAs for our
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.
Although linear exponential comonads are usually defined as comonads on symmetric monoidal categories, we can also define linear exponential comonads on non-symmetric monoidal categories
[
Has16
]
.
In
[
Tom22
]
, we defined
exponential relational planar linear combinatory algebras (exp-rPLCAs)
as pairs of a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra and an applicative endomorphism on it, that give rise to linear exponential comonads on (non-symmetric) monoidal bi-closed categories.
The definition of exp-rPLCAs in
[
Tom22
]
are the reformulation of the definition of (relational) LCAs to bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras.
In this paper, we generalize exp-rPLCAs a bit by changing “bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras” to “
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras,” and then similarly call the generalized ones as exp-rPLCAs.
New exp-rPLCAs give rise to linear exponential comonads on (non-symmetric) monoidal closed categories, and correspond to adjoint pairs of applicative morphisms between
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and PCAs.

There are also modalities on (non-symmetric) linear calculus other than the linear exponential modality.
The
exchange modality
, investigated in
[
JIdP18
]
, is a modality connecting a commutative logic and a non-commutative logic (the
Lambek calculus
).
Categorical models of the exchange modality are given as monoidal adjunctions between monoidal bi-closed categories and SMCCs, which are called
Lambek adjoint models
.
In
[
Tom22
]
, we defined
exchange relational planar linear combinatory algebras (exch-rPLCAs)
that give rise to Lambek adjoint models.
In this paper, like exp-rPLCAs, we reformulate exch-rPLCAs for
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.
New exch-rPLCAs correspond to adjoint pairs between
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras, and give rise to monoidal adjunctions between (non-symmetric) monoidal closed categories and SMCCs, that are models of the exchange modality based on the non-symmetric multiplicative intuitionistic linear logic (that is, a fragment of the Lambek calculus without bi-closedness).

Finally in section
7
and
8
, we discuss related work, summarize conclusion and describe future work.

2.
Background

2.1.
Applicative structures and categories of assemblies

First we recall basic notions of the categorical realizability.
Notations and definitions in this subsection are from
[
Lon95
]
.

{defi}

A
partial applicative structure

{\mathcal{A}}
is a pair of a set
|{\mathcal{A}}|
and a partial binary operation
(x,y)\mapsto x\cdot y
on
|{\mathcal{A}}|
.
When the binary operation is total, we say
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
total applicative structure
.

We often omit
\cdot
and write
x\cdot y
as
xy
simply.
We also omit unnecessary parentheses assuming that application joins from the left.
For instance,
xy(zw)
denotes
(x\cdot y)\cdot(z\cdot w)
.

In the sequel, we use two notations “
\downarrow
” and “
\simeq
.”
We write
xy\downarrow
for that
x\cdot y
is defined.
“
\simeq
” denotes the Kleene equality, which means that if the one side of the equation is defined then the other side is also defined and both sides are equal.

{defi}

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a partial applicative structure.

(1)

An
assembly
on
{\mathcal{A}}
is a pair
X=(|X|,\|\mathchar 45\|_{X})
, where
|X|
is a set and
\|\mathchar 45\|_{X}
is a function sending
x\in|X|
to a non-empty subset
\|{x}\|_{X}
of
|{\mathcal{A}}|
.
We call elements of
\|{x}\|_{X}

realizers of

x
.

(2)

For assemblies
X
and
Y
on
{\mathcal{A}}
, a
map of assemblies

f:X\rightarrow Y
is a function
f:|X|\rightarrow|Y|
such that there exists an element
r\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
realizing
f
.
Here we say “
r

realizes

f
” or “
r

is a realizer of

f
” if
r
satisfies that

\forall x\in|X|
,
\forall a\in\|{x}\|_{X}
,
ra\downarrow
and
ra\in\|{f(x)}\|_{Y}
.

If we assume two additional conditions on a partial applicative structure, we can construct two kinds of categories.

{defi}

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a partial applicative structure satisfying that:

(1)

|{\mathcal{A}}|
has an element
{\bf I}
such that
\forall x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf I}x\downarrow
and
{\bf I}x=x
;

(2)

for any
r_{1},r_{2}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
, there exists
r\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
rx\simeq r_{1}(r_{2}x)
.

Then we construct categories as follows.

(1)

The category
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
, called the
category of assemblies
on
{\mathcal{A}}
, consists of assemblies on
{\mathcal{A}}
as its objects and maps of assemblies as its maps.
Identity maps and composition maps are the same as those of
{\sf Sets}
(the category of sets and functions).

(2)

We call an assembly
X
a
modest set
on
{\mathcal{A}}
if
X
satisfies

\forall x,x^{\prime}\in|X|
,
x\neq x^{\prime}\Rightarrow\|{x}\|_{X}\cap\|{x^{\prime}}\|_{X}=\emptyset
.

The category
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
, called the
category of modest sets
on
{\mathcal{A}}
, is the full subcategory of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
whose objects are modest sets on
{\mathcal{A}}
.

We need above two conditions
1
and
2
to give realizers of the identities and composition maps.
Identities are realized by
{\bf I}
.
For maps
f_{1}:Y\rightarrow Z
realized by
r_{1}
and
f_{2}:X\rightarrow Y
realized by
r_{2}
, we obtain
r
given by the condition
2
, which realizes
f_{1}\circ f_{2}
.
Since all the classes of applicative structures introduced later satisfy these conditions, the conditions are not be much problems in this paper.

Intuitively, the category
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) can be understood as the category of “
{\mathcal{A}}
-computable universe.”
For an assembly
X=(|X|,\|\mathchar 45\|_{X})
on
{\mathcal{A}}
, elements of
\|{x}\|_{X}
can be seen as “machine-level interpretations” of
x\in|X|
.
For a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
, the realizer
r
of
f
can be seen as “machine implementation” of
f
, since
r
takes interpretations of
x
(that is, elements of
\|{x}\|_{X}
) as input and computes interpretations of
f(x)
(that is, elements of
\|{f(x)}\|_{Y}
).

2.2.
PCAs and Cartesian closed categories

Since
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is the category of
{\mathcal{A}}
-computable universe, the structure of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
depends on the computational structure of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
When applicative structures belong to a specific class, specific categorical structures may be found on the categories of assemblies.
The best known such class is the class of PCAs, which induce Cartesian closed categories of assemblies.
Results in this subsection are from
[
Lon95
]
.

{defi}

A
partial combinatory algebra (PCA)
is a partial applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}
which contains two special elements
{\bf S}
and
{\bf K}
such that:

•

\forall x,y\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf K}x\downarrow
,
{\bf K}xy\downarrow
and
{\bf K}xy=y
;

•

\forall x,y,z\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf S}x\downarrow
,
{\bf S}xy\downarrow
and
{\bf S}xyz\simeq xz(yz)
.

When a PCA
{\mathcal{A}}
is a total applicative structure, we say
{\mathcal{A}}
is an
{\bf SK}
-
algebra
.

The most fundamental example of PCAs is the untyped lambda calculus.

{exa}

Suppose infinite supply of variables
x,y,z,\dots
.
Untyped lambda terms
are terms constructed from the following six rules:

(identity)

x\vdash x

\Gamma\vdash M

\Delta\vdash N

(application)

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash MN

\Gamma,x\vdash M

(abstraction)

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M

\Gamma,x,y,\Delta\vdash M

(exchange)

\Gamma,y,x,\Delta\vdash M

\Gamma,x,y\vdash M

(contraction)

\Gamma,x\vdash M[x/y]

\Gamma\vdash M

(weakening)

\Gamma,x\vdash M

Here, in the application rule,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are sequences of distinct variables and contain no common variables.
In the contraction rule,
M[x/y]
denotes the term obtained by substituting
x
for all free
y
in
M
.
In the weakening rule,
x
is a variable not contained in
\Gamma
.

Note that abstraction rules are only applied to the rightmost variables. In order to
apply the abstraction rule to a variable in a different position, we need to use the exchange rule several times and move the variable to the rightmost place.

We define
\beta
-
equivalence relation

=_{\beta}
on lambda terms as the congruence of the relation
(\lambda x.M)N\sim M[N/x]
.
Untyped lambda terms modulo
=_{\beta}
form a PCA (actually an
{\bf SK}
-algebra). The underlying set of the PCA consists of
\beta
-equivalence classes of untyped closed lambda terms (i.e., lambda terms with no free variables) and the application is defined as that of lambda terms. In this example,
\lambda xyz.xz(yz)
is the representative of
{\bf S}
and
\lambda xy.x
is the representative of
{\bf K}
.

The correspondence between PCAs and the lambda calculus is more than just an example. PCAs have an important property called the
combinatory completeness
, which gives interpretations of “computable functions” on
{\mathcal{A}}
by elements of
{\mathcal{A}}
itself.
First, we give the definition of polynomials over an applicative structure (not restricted to PCAs).

{defi}

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a partial applicative structure.
A
polynomial
over
{\mathcal{A}}
is a syntactic expression generated by variables, elements of
|{\mathcal{A}}|
and the application of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
For two polynomials
M
and
N
over
{\mathcal{A}}
,
M\simeq N
means that

M[a_{1}/x_{1},\dots,a_{n}/x_{n}]\simeq N[a_{1}/x_{1},\dots,a_{n}/x_{n}]

holds in
{\mathcal{A}}
for any
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
, where
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
contains all the variables of
M
and
N
.

Proposition 1

(combinatory completeness for PCAs)
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a PCA and
M
be a polynomial over
{\mathcal{A}}
.
For any variable
x
, there exists a polynomial
M^{\prime}
such that the free variables of
M^{\prime}
are the free variables of
M
excluding
x
and
M^{\prime}a\simeq M[a/x]
holds for all
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
.
We write such
M^{\prime}
as
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.M
.

Proof 2.1
.

We define
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.M
by induction on the structure of
M
.

•

{\lambda}^{\ast}x.x:={\bf S}{\bf K}{\bf K}

•

{\lambda}^{\ast}x.y:={\bf K}y
(when
x\neq y
)

•

{\lambda}^{\ast}x.MN:={\bf S}({\lambda}^{\ast}x.M)({\lambda}^{\ast}x.N)

For the special case of the above proposition, any closed lambda term is
\beta
-equivalent to some term constructed from
\lambda xy.x
and
\lambda xyz.xz(yz)
using applications.

Using the combinatory completeness, we can give
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) on a PCA
{\mathcal{A}}
the structure of Cartesian closed category (CCC).

Proposition 2
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}
is a PCA,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
are CCCs.

While this result is standard, we shall outline its proof for comparison with the parallel results on various classes of combinatory algebras to be developed in this paper.

Proof 2.2
.

First we prove the proposition for
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.
Let
{\mathbb{C}}:=\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.

•

By the combinatory completeness,
|{\mathcal{A}}|
has elements
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.x
and
{\lambda}^{\ast}xyz.x(yz)
, which make
{\mathcal{A}}
satisfying the conditions
1
and
2
of Definition
2.1
.
Thus
{\mathbb{C}}
is a category.

•

For objects
X
and
Y
, the underlying set of the binary product
X\times Y
is
|X|\times|Y|
.
Realizers are defined as

\|{(x,y)}\|_{X\times Y}:=\{{\lambda}^{\ast}t.tpq\mid p\in\|{x}\|_{X},q\in\|{y}\|_{Y}\}.

•

For maps
f:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
realized by
r_{f}
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
realized by
r_{g}
,
f\times g
is the function sending
(x,y)
to
(f(x),g(y))
.
A realizer for
f\times g
does exists as
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.u({\lambda}^{\ast}pqt.t(r_{f}p)(r_{g}q))
.

•

The underlying set of the terminal object
1
is the singleton
\{\ast\}
.
Realizers are
\|{\ast}\|_{1}:=|{\mathcal{A}}|
.
It is easy to see that this
1
satisfy the conditions of the terminal object.

•

The projection
\pi:X\times Y\rightarrow X
is the function sending
(x,y)
to
x
and has a realizer
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.u({\lambda}^{\ast}pq.p)
.
The projection
\pi^{\prime}:X\times Y\rightarrow Y
is the function sending
(x,y)
to
y
and has a realizer
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.u({\lambda}^{\ast}pq.q)
.
It is easy to see that these
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
satisfy the conditions of the projections of the Cartesian category.

•

For objects
X
and
Y
, the underlying set of the exponential
Y^{X}
is
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
.
Realizers are

\|{f}\|_{Y^{X}}:=\{r\in|{\mathcal{A}}|\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}.

•

For maps
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
realized by
r_{f}
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
realized by
r_{g}
, the map
g^{f}
is the function sending a map
h\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
realized by
r_{h}
to
g\circ h\circ f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}v.r_{g}(r_{h}(r_{f}v))
.
A realizer of
g^{f}
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}uv.r_{g}(u(r_{f}v))
.

•

The adjunction
\Phi:{\mathbb{C}}(X\times Y,Z)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}(X,Z^{Y})
is the function sending
f:X\times Y\rightarrow Z
realized by
r_{f}
to the map
\Phi(f):x\mapsto(y\mapsto f(x,y))
.

\Phi(f)
is realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}pq.r_{f}({\lambda}^{\ast}t.tpq)
.
For a map
g:X\rightarrow Z^{Y}
realized by
r_{g}
,
\Phi^{-1}(g):X\times Y\rightarrow Z
is the map sending
(x,y)
to
g(x)(y)
.

\Phi^{-1}(g)
is realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.ur_{g}
.
It is easy to see that this
\Phi
satisfies the condition of the adjunction of the CCC.

Therefore,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is a CCC.

Next we show that
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
is a CCC.

Given modest sets
X
and
Y
on
{\mathcal{A}}
, we define the binary product
X\times Y
in the same way as
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.
Here we can show that
X\times Y
also is a modest set.
Suppose there is some
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
realizing different
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
of
|X|\times|Y|
.
When we assume
x\neq x^{\prime}
, though
\pi(x,y)\neq\pi(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
, both sides have the same realizer
({\lambda}^{\ast}u.u({\lambda}^{\ast}pq.p))a
.
It contradicts that
X
is a modest set.
The same contradiction is lead when
y\neq y^{\prime}
.
Therefore, different
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
do not have common realizers and
X\times Y
is a modest set.

For modest sets
X
and
Y
on
{\mathcal{A}}
, we also define
Y^{X}
in the same way as
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.
We can show that
Y^{X}
also is a modest set.
Suppose there is some
r
realizing different
f:X\rightarrow Y
and
g:X\rightarrow Y
.
Take
x\in|X|
and
a\in\|{x}\|_{X}
such that
f(x)\neq g(x)
.
Then
ra
is an element of both
\|{f(x)}\|_{Y}
and
\|{g(x)}\|_{Y}
.
However, it contradicts that
Y
is a modest set.
Therefore,
Y^{X}
is a modest set.

Hence, we can show that
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
is a CCC by the same proof for
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.

In this proof, we use the combinatory completeness for the PCA a lot to give realizers for each assembly and map.

2.3.
{\bf BCI}
-algebras and symmetric monoidal closed categories

Given an applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}
which has the different computational structure from PCAs, we obtain
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
with a different categorical structure from CCCs.
In this subsection, we recall another well-known class of applicative structures called
{\bf BCI}
-algebras, which correspond to linear structures.
Results given in this subsection are from
[
AL05
,
Hos07
]
.

{defi}

A
{\bf BCI}
-
algebra
is a total applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}
which contains three elements
{\bf B}
,
{\bf C}
and
{\bf I}
such that
\forall x,y,z\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf B}xyz=x(yz)
,
{\bf C}xyz=xzy
and
{\bf I}x=x
.

{exa}

Untyped linear lambda terms
are untyped lambda terms constructed without using weakening and contraction rules (See Example
2.2
).
That is, an untyped linear lambda term is an untyped lambda term whose each variable appears just once in the term.
Untyped closed linear lambda terms modulo
=_{\beta}
form a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.
Here
\lambda xyz.x(yz)
,
\lambda xyz.xzy
and
\lambda x.x
are the representatives of
{\bf B}
,
{\bf C}
and
{\bf I}
respectively.

Proposition 3

(combinatory completeness for
{\bf BCI}
-algebras)
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra and
M
be a polynomial over
{\mathcal{A}}
.
For any variable
x
appearing exactly once in
M
, there exists a polynomial
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.M
such that the free variables of
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.M
are the free variables of
M
excluding
x
and
({\lambda}^{\ast}x.M)a=M[a/x]
for all
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
.

Proof 2.3
.

We define
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.M
by induction on the structure of
M
.

•

{\lambda}^{\ast}x.x:={\bf I}

•

{\lambda}^{\ast}x.MN:=\begin{cases}{\bf C}({\lambda}^{\ast}x.M)N&(x\in FV(M))\\
{\bf B}M({\lambda}^{\ast}x.N)&(x\in FV(N))\end{cases}

The combinatory completeness for a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra allows interpreting only linear lambda terms, not the whole of lambda terms.
Thus some realizers used in the proof of Proposition
2
(such as
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.u({\lambda}^{\ast}pq.p)
) may not exist in a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.
For
{\bf BCI}
-algebras, the categories of assemblies have other categorical structure than CCCs.

Proposition 4
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is a symmetric monoidal closed category (SMCC).

Proof 2.4

(Proof sketch)
.

•

For objects
X
and
Y
, the underlying set of
X\otimes Y
is
|X|\times|Y|
.
Realizers are defined as
\|{x\otimes y}\|_{X\otimes Y}:=\{{\lambda}^{\ast}t.tpq\mid p\in\|{x}\|_{X},q\in\|{y}\|_{Y}\}
.

•

For maps
f:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
realized by
r_{f}
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
realized by
r_{g}
, the map
f\otimes g
is the function sending
x\otimes y
to
f(x)\otimes g(y)
,
which is realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.u({\lambda}^{\ast}pqt.t(r_{f}p)(r_{g}q))
.

•

The underlying set of the unit object
I
is the singleton
\{\ast\}
.
The realizer is
\|{\ast}\|_{I}:=\{{\bf I}\}
.

•

The right unitor
\rho_{X}:X\rightarrow X\otimes I
is the function sending
x
to
x\otimes\ast
, which is realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}p.({\lambda}^{\ast}t.tp{\bf I})
.
The inverse
\rho^{-1}
is realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.u({\lambda}^{\ast}pq.qp)
.

•

Also we can take the left unitor
\lambda_{X}:I\otimes X\rightarrow X
as the function
(\ast\otimes x)\mapsto x
and the associator

\alpha_{XYZ}:X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)\rightarrow(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z
as
x\otimes(y\otimes z)\mapsto(x\otimes y)\otimes z
.

•

The symmetry
\sigma_{XY}:X\otimes Y\rightarrow Y\otimes X
is the function sending
x\otimes y
to
y\otimes x
, which is realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.u({\lambda}^{\ast}pqt.tqp)
.

•

For objects
X
and
Y
, the underlying set of the exponential
1
1

1

For an SMCC
{\mathbb{C}}
, the exponential is often denoted using the symbol
\multimap
satisfying

{\mathbb{C}}(X\otimes Y,Z)\cong{\mathbb{C}}(X,Y\multimap Z).

However, here we use the reversed symbol

\multimap

satisfying
{\mathbb{C}}(X\otimes Y,Z)\cong{\mathbb{C}}(X,Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y)
to be consistent with the notation of monoidal bi-closed categories in Section
3.4
.

Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X
is
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})}(X,Y)
.
Realizers are
\|{f}\|_{Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X}:=\{r\in|{\mathcal{A}}|\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}
.

•

For maps
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
realized by
r_{f}
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
realized by
r_{g}
, the map
g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f
is the function sending
h:X\rightarrow Y
to
g\circ h\circ f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
.
A realizer of
g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}uv.r_{g}(u(r_{f}v))
.

•

The adjunction
\Phi
sends a map
f:X\otimes Y\rightarrow Z
to the map
\Phi(f):x\mapsto(y\mapsto f(x\otimes y))
.

It is easy to see that the above components satisfy the axioms of the SMCC.

The above proof is almost the same as the proof of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
on a PCA
{\mathcal{A}}
being a CCC.
However, when we prove that
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
on a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is an SMCC, we cannot use the same proof as for PCAs.
That is because for modest sets
X
and
Y
on a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
,
X\otimes Y
given by the same way as
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is not generally a modest set.
The following proposition is proven with a modification to resolve the problem.

Proposition 5
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra,
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
is an SMCC.

Proof 2.5
.

Let
G:\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})\hookrightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
be the inclusion functor and
F:\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
be the left adjoint of
G
.

F
is the functor sending an assembly
X=(|X|,\|\mathchar 45\|_{X})
to a modest set
Z=(|X|/\approx,\|\mathchar 45\|_{Z})
.
Here the relation “
\approx
” is the transitive closure of the relation “
\sim
” defined as
x\sim x^{\prime}

:\Leftrightarrow

\|{x}\|_{X}\cap\|{x^{\prime}}\|_{X}\neq\emptyset
.
The realizers of
z\in|Z|
are defined as
\|{z}\|_{Z}:=\bigcup_{x\in z}\|{x}\|_{X}
.

F
sends a map
f
of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
to the canonical map of
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
, which is realized by realizers of
f
.

We define the tensor product
\boxtimes
in
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
as
X\boxtimes Y:=F(GX\otimes GY)
.
We can prove Proposition
5
by the same proof of Proposition
4
by replacing
\otimes
to
\boxtimes
.

More general about constructing monoidal structures on reflexive full subcategories, see
[
Day72
]
.

Remark 6
.

While we define
{\bf BCI}
-algebras as a class of total applicative structures, we also can define “partial
{\bf BCI}
-algebras” naturally.
For a partial
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, we can see that:

•

\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is not generally an SMCC;

•

adding an extra element
\bot
(which means “undefined”),
{\mathcal{A}}
naturally extends to a total
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\bot}
;

•

\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is the full subcategory of
\sf{Asm}({{\mathcal{A}}_{\bot}})
.

The same discussion is given in
[
Hos07
]
.

2.4.
Applicative morphisms

In this subsection, we recall the notion of applicative morphisms from
[
Lon95
]
.

{defi}

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a partial applicative structure satisfying:

(1)

|{\mathcal{A}}|
has an element
{\bf I}
such that
\forall x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf I}x\downarrow
and
{\bf I}x=x
;

(2)

for any
r_{1},r_{2}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
, there exists
r\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
rx\simeq r_{1}(r_{2}x)
.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}
be another partial applicative structure satisfying the same conditions.
An
applicative morphism

\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
is a total relation from
|{\mathcal{A}}|
to
|{\mathcal{B}}|
such that there exists a
realizer

r_{\gamma}\in|{\mathcal{B}}|
of
\gamma
satisfying that

\forall a,a^{\prime}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
\forall b\in\gamma a
,
\forall b^{\prime}\in\gamma a^{\prime}
,
r_{\gamma}bb^{\prime}\in\gamma(aa^{\prime})
whenever
aa^{\prime}\downarrow
.

We say
\gamma
is
functional
when
\gamma a
is a singleton for each
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
, and simply write
\gamma a=b
for
\gamma a=\{b\}
.

Our definition is slightly more general than the definition in
[
Lon95
]
that makes sense only on PCAs.
We define applicative morphisms between applicative structures satisfying the conditions of Definition
2.1
.
We assume these conditions to realize identity and composition morphisms.
By the condition
1
, the identity applicative morphism
id:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
can be realized by
{\bf I}
.
For applicative morphisms
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
and
\delta:{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}
realized by
r_{\gamma}
and
r_{\delta}
, taking
p\in\delta r_{\gamma}
, the composition
\delta\circ\gamma
can be realized by
r\in|{\mathcal{B}}|
such that
\forall b\in|{\mathcal{B}}|
,
rb\simeq r_{\delta}(r_{\delta}pb)
.
The condition
2
gives such a realizer
r
.

In the sequel, for an applicative morphism
\gamma
, when we write an indexed element
r_{\gamma}
, it denotes a realizer of
\gamma
.
Also, for
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
and
S,S^{\prime}\subseteq|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
when we write
aS
, it denotes the set
\{as\mid s\in S\}
and we consider
as\downarrow
for all
s\in S
,
and when we write
SS^{\prime}
, it denotes the set

\{ss^{\prime}\mid s\in S,s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}\}
and we consider
ss^{\prime}\downarrow
for all
s\in S
and
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
.
For instance, the condition that
\gamma
is an applicative morphism is denoted as

\exists r_{\gamma}\in|{\mathcal{B}}|,\forall a,a^{\prime}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
aa^{\prime}\downarrow\Rightarrow r_{\gamma}(\gamma a)(\gamma a^{\prime})\subseteq\gamma(aa^{\prime})
.

From applicative morphisms, we can obtain functors between the categories of assemblies.

{defi}

For an applicative morphism
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
,
{\gamma}_{\ast}:\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{B}})
is the functor sending an object
(|X|,\|\mathchar 45\|_{X})
to
(|X|,\gamma\|\mathchar 45\|_{X})
and sending a map to the same function.

For a map
f
in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
realized by
r_{f}
,
{\gamma}_{\ast}f
is realized by elements of
r_{\gamma}(\gamma r_{f})
.
It is obvious that
{\gamma}_{\ast}
satisfies
{\gamma}_{\ast}(id)=id
and
{\gamma}_{\ast}(g\circ f)={\gamma}_{\ast}(g)\circ{\gamma}_{\ast}(f)
.

Next we recall the preorder relation
\preceq
between applicative morphisms.

{defi}

For two applicative morphisms
\gamma,\delta:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
,
\gamma\preceq\delta
iff there is
r\in|{\mathcal{B}}|
such that
\forall a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
r(\gamma a)\subseteq\delta a
.

Using the conditions
1
and
2
of Definition
2.4
, we can easily show that
\preceq
is a preorder.

By the preorder
\preceq
, we can define adjunctions and comonads on applicative structures.

{defi}

For two applicative morphisms
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
and
\delta:{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
,
\gamma
is a
right adjoint
of
\delta
iff
\delta\circ\gamma\preceq id_{{\mathcal{A}}}
and
id_{{\mathcal{B}}}\preceq\gamma\circ\delta
.
We write
(\delta\dashv\gamma):{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
for these settings.

{defi}

An applicative morphism
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
is called
comonadic
when
|{\mathcal{A}}|
has two elements
{\bf e}
and
{\bf d}
such that
\forall a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf e}(\gamma a)\subseteq\{a\}
and
{\bf d}(\gamma a)\subseteq\gamma(\gamma a)
.

For adjunctions of applicative morphisms, the following properties hold.

Proposition 7
.

(1)

An adjoint pair of applicative morphisms
(\delta\dashv\gamma):{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
gives rise to an adjoint pair
({\delta}_{\ast}\dashv{\gamma}_{\ast}):\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{B}})
.

(2)

For an adjoint pair of applicative morphisms
(\delta\dashv\gamma):{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
,
\delta\circ\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
is a comonadic applicative morphism.

(3)

For a comonadic applicative morphism
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
,
{\gamma}_{\ast}
is a comonad on
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.

Remark 8
.

In Definition
2.4
, an applicative morphism
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
gives rise to the functor
{\gamma}_{\ast}:\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{B}})
.
However, here we cannot generally obtain a functor
{\gamma}_{\ast}:\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{B}})
since
\|{x}\|_{X}\cap\|{x^{\prime}}\|_{X}=\emptyset
does not imply
\gamma(\|{x}\|_{X})\cap\gamma(\|{x^{\prime}}\|_{X})=\emptyset
and
{\gamma}_{\ast}X
may not be in
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{B}})
.
However, for a comonadic applicative morphism
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
,
{\gamma}_{\ast}
can be restricted to the endofunctor on
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
.
Indeed, for a modest set
X
on
{\mathcal{A}}
, if
a\in\gamma(\|{x}\|_{X})\cap\gamma(\|{x^{\prime}}\|_{X})
then

{\bf e}a
is an element of
\|{x}\|_{X}\cap\|{x^{\prime}}\|_{X}
and thus
x=x^{\prime}
concludes.
Furthermore, this
{\gamma}_{\ast}
is a comonad on
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
.

2.5.
Linear combinatory algebras

In the previous subsection, we saw comonadic applicative morphisms give rise to comonads, and adjoint pairs of applicative morphisms give rise to adjoint pairs between categories of assemblies.
Using this construction, we can obtain linear exponential comonads and linear-non-linear models for the linear logic.
In this subsection, we recall notions of linear combinatory algebras (LCAs) from
[
AHS02
]
and relational linear combinatory algebras (rLCAs) from
[
Hos07
]
.

{defi}

A
linear combinatory algebra (LCA)
consists of:

•

a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
;

•

a functional comonadic applicative morphism
(\mathbbold{\exm},{\bf e},{\bf d})
on
{\mathcal{A}}
;

•

an element
{\bf k}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x,y\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf k}x(\mathbbold{\exm}y)=x
;

•

an element
{\bf w}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x,y\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf w}x(\mathbbold{\exm}y)=x(\mathbbold{\exm}y)(\mathbbold{\exm}y)
.

As we get comonads from comonadic applicative morphism, from LCAs, we get linear exponential comonads, which are categorical models of the linear exponential modality of the linear logic.

{defi}

Let
{\mathbb{C}}
be a symmetric monoidal category.
A
linear exponential comonad
consists of the following data.

•

A symmetric monoidal comonad
(!,\delta,\epsilon,m,m_{I})
.

Here
!
is an endofunctor on
{\mathbb{C}}
,

\delta_{X}:!X\rightarrow!!X
and
\epsilon_{X}:!X\rightarrow X
are monoidal natural transformations for the comultiplication and the counit.
The natural transformation
m_{X,Y}:!X\otimes!Y\rightarrow!(X\otimes Y)
and the map
m_{I}:I\rightarrow!I
make
!
be a monoidal functor.

•

Monoidal natural transformations
e_{X}:!X\rightarrow I
and
d_{X}:!X\rightarrow!X\otimes!X
.

Here these components need satisfy the following conditions for each
X
.

(1)

(!X,d_{X},e_{X})
is a commutative comonoid in
{\mathbb{C}}
.

(2)

e_{X}
and
d_{X}
are coalgebra morphisms.

(3)

\delta_{X}
is a comonoid morphism.

Proposition 9
.

For an LCA
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
,
{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}
is a linear exponential comonad on the SMCC
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(or
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
).

LCAs can be generalized from functional applicative morphisms to not functional ones, called rLCAs.

{defi}

A
relational linear combinatory algebra (rLCA)
consists of:

•

a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
;

•

a comonadic applicative morphism
(\mathbbold{\exm},{\bf e},{\bf d})
on
{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\mathbbold{\exm}\preceq[\mathbbold{\exm},\mathbbold{\exm}]
and
\mathbbold{\exm}\preceq k_{i}
.
Here
[\mathbbold{\exm},\mathbbold{\exm}]
and
k_{i}
are applicative morphisms defined as
[\mathbbold{\exm},\mathbbold{\exm}](x):=\{{\lambda}^{\ast}t.taa^{\prime}\mid a,a^{\prime}\in\mathbbold{\exm}x\}
and
k_{i}(x):=\{{\bf I}\}
.

Next proposition shows the correspondence between LCAs, rLCAs and adjoint pairs between
{\bf BCI}
-algebras and PCAs.

Proposition 10
.

(1)

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra and
{\mathcal{B}}
be a PCA.
For an adjoint pair

(\delta\dashv\gamma):{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
,
({\mathcal{A}},\delta\circ\gamma)
is an rLCA.

(2)

Let
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
be an LCA.
The applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbbold{\exm}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,@)
defined by
x@y:=x(\mathbbold{\exm}y)
is a PCA.
Furthermore,
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbbold{\exm}}
defined as the identity function and
\delta:{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbbold{\exm}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
sending
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
to
\mathbbold{\exm}a
form an adjoint pair
(\delta\dashv\gamma):{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbbold{\exm}}
.

From rLCAs, we also get linear exponential comonads.
Moreover, we get linear-non-linear models
[
Ben95
]
on categories of assemblies or categories of modest sets.

{defi}

A
linear-non-linear model
is a symmetric monoidal adjunction

(F\dashv G):{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{D}}
for an SMCC
{\mathbb{C}}
and a CCC
{\mathbb{D}}
.

Proposition 11
.

For an rLCA
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
,
{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}
is a linear exponential comonad on the SMCC
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(or
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
).
Furthermore, the co-Kleisli adjunction between
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}}
(or
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})_{\mathbbold{\exm}}
) is symmetric monoidal.
Thus the adjunction forms a linear-non-linear model.

3.
Constructing non-symmetric categorical structures

In section
2
, we saw two known results that PCAs/
{\bf BCI}
-algebras induce CCCs/SMCCs as the categories of assemblies and the categories of modest sets.
It is natural to try to extend these results to other classes of applicative structures, and we introduce such new classes inducing certain “non-symmetric” categorical structures.
In this section we recall
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras,
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras from
[
Tom21
,
Tom22
]
, and introduce a new class
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

3.1.
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and closed multicategories

When we try to obtain some non-symmetric categorical structures on categories of assemblies, we will find a subtle problem.
In a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, the
{\bf C}
-combinator expresses exchanging the order of arguments, and is the source of the symmetric structures of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.
So one might guess that simply omitting
{\bf C}
would be sufficient for getting a non-symmetric categorical structure on
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.
However, this does not work well;
{\bf B}
and
{\bf I}
alone are too weak to give an interesting structure on
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.

For instance, if we want the internal hom functor
(-\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>-)
on
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
on a total applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}
, we need certain exchanging operation in
{\mathcal{A}}
even if the closed structure is not symmetric.
Take an object
A
of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
as
|A|:=|{\mathcal{A}}|
and
\|{a}\|_{A}:=\{a\}
.
For maps
f,g:A\rightarrow A
, to realize
g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f
, we need a realizer
r
which satisfies
\forall a,a^{\prime}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
raa^{\prime}=r_{g}(a(r_{f}a^{\prime}))
.
This
r
acts as the exchanging to move the information of
r_{f}
from the left of
a
to the right of
a
.
(In a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra, such
r
exists as
{\bf C}({\bf B}{\bf B}({\bf B}r_{g}))r_{f}
.)

Therefore, when we want some non-symmetric categorical structures such as non-symmetric closed structures, we need to prepare some “more restricted exchanging” than the
{\bf C}
-combinator.
One way to resolve the problem is to supply not a combinator but the unary operation
{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
for exchanging.
In this subsection, we introduce
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras from
[
Tom21
]
, which induce non-symmetric closed multicategories.

{defi}

A total applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-
algebra
iff it contains
{\bf B}
,
{\bf I}
and
{a}^{\bullet}
for each
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
, where
{a}^{\bullet}
is an element of
|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{a}^{\bullet}x=xa
.

This
{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
enable restricted exchanges than the
{\bf C}
-combinator.
Since in a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra,
{\bf C}{\bf I}a
satisfies the axiom of
{a}^{\bullet}
, all
{\bf BCI}
-algebras are also
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

The definition of
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras may seem strange compared to the definitions of PCAs or
{\bf BCI}
-algebras.
However, the definition of
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras is natural in the aspect of having a good correspondence with the “planar” lambda calculus.

{exa}

Untyped planar lambda terms
are untyped lambda terms constructed without using weakening, contraction nor exchange rules (See Example
2.2
).
That is, untyped planar lambda terms are untyped linear lambda terms such that for each subterm
\lambda x.M
,
x
is the rightmost free variable of
M
.
Untyped closed planar lambda terms modulo
=_{\beta}
form a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, which we call
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
in this paper.
Here
\lambda xyz.x(yz)
and
\lambda x.x
are the representatives of
{\bf B}
and
{\bf I}
respectively. Given a representative
M
of
a\in|{\mathcal{L}}_{P}|
,
\lambda x.xM
is also a closed planar term and is the representative of
{a}^{\bullet}
.

Remark 12
.

The definition of construction rules of planar lambda terms has two different styles. In our definition, the abstraction rule is only allowed for the rightmost variable. Such a style is seen in
[
Abr09
]
. On the other hand, there is also the definition that the abstraction rule is only allowed for the leftmost variable, as in
[
ZG15
]
. Here we employ the former style for preservation the planarity of terms under the
\beta\eta
-conversions.

Proposition 13

(combinatory completeness for
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras)
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra and
M
be a polynomial over
|{\mathcal{A}}|
.
For the rightmost variable
x
of
M
, if
x
appears exactly once in
M
, there exists a polynomial
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.M
such that the free variables of
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.M
are the free variables of
M
excluding
x
and
({\lambda}^{\ast}x.M)a=M[a/x]
for all
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
.

Proof 3.1
.

We define
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.M
by induction on the structure of
M
.

•

{\lambda}^{\ast}x.x:={\bf I}

•

{\lambda}^{\ast}x.MN:=\begin{cases}{\bf B}{N}^{\bullet}({\lambda}^{\ast}x.M)&(x\in FV(M))\\
{\bf B}M({\lambda}^{\ast}x.N)&(x\in FV(N))\end{cases}

Note that for
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.MN
,
x
is the rightmost free variable in
MN
, and thus, if
x
is in
FV(M)
,
N
has no free variables and
{N}^{\bullet}
can be defined.

Then we show
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras induce certain categorical structures on the categories of assemblies.
First we recall the definition of closed multicategories from
[
Man12
]
.

{defi}

A
multicategory

{\mathbb{C}}
consists of the following data:

(1)

a collection
Ob({\mathbb{C}})
;

(2)

for each
n\geq 0
and
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n},Y\in Ob({\mathbb{C}})
, a set
{\mathbb{C}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)
.
We often write
f\in{\mathbb{C}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)
as
f:X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\rightarrow Y
;

(3)

for each
X\in Ob({\mathbb{C}})
, an element
id_{X}\in{\mathbb{C}}(X;X)
, called the
identity map
;

(4)

for each
n,m_{1},m_{2},\dots,m_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
and
X^{k}_{j},Y_{k},Z

(1\leq k\leq n,1\leq j\leq m_{k})
, a function

\circ:{\mathbb{C}}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n};Z)\times\prod_{k}^{n}{\mathbb{C}}(X^{k}_{1},\dots,X^{k}_{m_{k}};Y_{k})\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}(X^{1}_{1},\dots,X^{1}_{m_{1}},X^{2}_{1},\dots,X^{n}_{m_{n}};Z)

called the
composition
.
g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
denotes the composition of
g\in{\mathbb{C}}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n};Z)
and
f_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}(X^{k}_{1},\dots,X^{k}_{m_{k}};Y_{k})

(1\leq k\leq n)
.
The compositions satisfy associativity and identity axioms.

{defi}

A
closed multicategory
consists of the following data:

(1)

a multicategory
{\mathbb{C}}
;

(2)

for each
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n},Y\in Ob({\mathbb{C}})
, an object
\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n};Y)
, called the
internal hom object
;

(3)

for each
X_{1},\dots,X_{n},Y\in Ob({\mathbb{C}})
, a map

ev_{X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y}:\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y),X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\rightarrow Y
,

called the
evaluation map
such that
\forall Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots,Z_{m}\in Ob({\mathbb{C}})
, the function

{\phi}_{Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y}:{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m};\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y))\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m},X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)

sending
f
to
ev_{X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y}\circ(f,id_{X_{1}},\dots,id_{X_{n}})
is invertible.
We write the inverse function
\Lambda_{Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y}
.

Remark 14
.

Here our definition of closed multicategories is different from the original definition in
[
Man12
]
in that the order of objects of domain of maps are reversed.
This is for ease to read by matching the orders of objects and realizers.

Proposition 15
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
are closed multicategories.

Proof 3.2
.

Let
{\mathbb{C}}:=\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.
Since
{\mathcal{A}}
have the
{\bf B}
-combinator and the
{\bf I}
-combinator,
{\mathbb{C}}
is a category.

First we give a bi-functor
(-\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>-):{\mathbb{C}}^{op}\times{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
as follows:

•

For
X,Y\in Ob({\mathbb{C}})
,
Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X
is an assembly whose underlying set is
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
and
\|{f}\|_{Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X}:=\{r\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}
.

•

For two maps
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
in
{\mathbb{C}}
,
(g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f):(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\rightarrow(Y^{\prime}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X^{\prime})
is the function sending
h\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
to
g\circ h\circ f
.

Given realizers
r_{f}
of
f
and
r_{g}
of
g
,
(g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f)
is realized by

{\lambda}^{\ast}uv.r_{g}(u(r_{f}v))
.
Thus, for any maps
f
and
g
in
{\mathbb{C}}
,
(g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f)
certainly is a map of
{\mathbb{C}}
.
It is easy to see that
(-\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>-)
preserves identities and compositions.

Next we give
{\mathbb{C}}
the structure of closed multicategory.

•

For an object
X\in Ob({\mathbb{C}})
,
{\mathbb{C}}(;X):=|X|
and
\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(;X):=X
.

•

For objects
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n},Y\in Ob({\mathbb{C}})
(
n\geq 1
), we define the internal hom object

\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y):=(\dots((Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X_{n})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X_{n-1})\dots)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X_{1}

and
{\mathbb{C}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)
is the underlying set of
\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)
.
We write
f(x_{1})(x_{2})\dots(x_{n})
as
f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
for
f\in{\mathbb{C}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)
and
x_{i}\in|X_{i}|
.

•

Identity maps
id_{X}\in{\mathbb{C}}(X;X)
(
X\in Ob({\mathbb{C}})
) are the same as identity maps of
{\mathbb{C}}
.

•

Suppose maps
g\in{\mathbb{C}}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n};Z)
and
f_{k}\in{\mathbb{C}}(X^{k}_{1},\dots,X^{k}_{m_{k}};Y_{k})
(
1\leq k\leq n
).
We define
g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
as the function that receives

x^{1}_{1},\dots,x^{1}_{m_{1}},\dots,x^{n}_{1},\dots,x^{n}_{m_{n}}

and returns
g(f_{1}(x^{1}_{1},\dots,x^{1}_{m_{1}}),\dots,f_{n}(x^{n}_{1},\dots,x^{n}_{m_{n}}))
.
Here when
m_{i}=0
for some
1\leq i\leq n
, we define

g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
by giving
y_{i}\in|Y_{i}|
pointed by
f_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}(;Y_{i})
as the
i
-th argument of
g
.
Given realizers
q\in\|{g}\|_{\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n};Z)}
and
p_{k}\in\|{f_{k}}\|_{\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X^{k}_{1},\dots,X^{k}_{m_{k}};Y_{k})}
, by the combinatory completeness for
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras, there is
r\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that

ra^{1}_{1}\dots a^{1}_{m_{1}}\dots a^{n}_{1}\dots a^{n}_{m_{n}}=q(p_{1}a^{1}_{1}\dots a^{1}_{m_{1}})\dots(p_{n}a^{n}_{1}\dots a^{n}_{m_{n}})

holds for any
a^{1}_{1},\dots,a^{n}_{m_{n}}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
.
This
r
realizes
g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
and thus
g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
is in
{\mathbb{C}}(X^{1}_{1},\dots,X^{n}_{m_{n}};Z)
.

•

The evaluation map
ev_{X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y}:\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y),X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\rightarrow Y
is given as the function that receives
f,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
and returns
f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, which is realized by
{\bf I}
.
Then,
\phi_{Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y}
is invertible as a function and for
g\in{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m},X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)
,
\Lambda(g)
is indeed in
{\mathbb{C}}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m};\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y))
since it is realized by realizers of
g
.

Therefore,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is a closed multicateogry.
For
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
, we can use the same proof as for
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.

While we define
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras as a class of total applicative structures, we also can define “partial
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra” naturally.
For a partial
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
,
{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
is a total unary operation on
|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall a,x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|,{a}^{\bullet}x\simeq xa
.
Unlike the case of partial
{\bf BCI}
-algebras as in Remark
6
, the proof of Proposition
15
is applicable to the case of partial
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

3.2.
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and closed categories

In this subsection, we recall a class of applicative structures from
[
Tom21
]
, which induce closed categories of assemblies and modest sets.
First we recall the definition of closed categories in
[
Man12
]
.

{defi}

A
closed category
consists of the following data:

(1)

a locally small category
{\mathbb{C}}
;

(2)

a functor
(-\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>-):{{\mathbb{C}}}^{op}\times{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
, called the
internal hom functor
2
2

2

While the internal hom object in the closed category is often written as
\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
,
[X,Y]
or
X\multimap Y
, here we denote
Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X
to be consistent with other categorical structures in this paper.
;

(3)

an object
I
, called the
unit object
;

(4)

a natural isomorphism
i_{X}:(X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I)\rightarrow X
;

(5)

an extranatural transformation
j_{X}:I\rightarrow(X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)
;

(6)

a transformation
L_{Y,Z}^{X}:(Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y)\rightarrow((Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X))
natural in
Y
and
Z
and extranatural in
X
,

such that the following axioms hold:

(1)

\forall X,Y\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
L_{Y,Y}^{X}\circ j_{Y}=j_{(Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}
;

(2)

\forall X,Y\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
i_{(Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}\circ(id_{(Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>j_{X})\circ L_{X,Y}^{X}=id_{(Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}
;

(3)

\forall X,Y,Z,W\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the following diagram commutes:
\textstyle{(W\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{Z,W}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{L_{Z,W}^{Y}}
\textstyle{(W\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{(Z\scalebox{0.6}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X),(W\scalebox{0.6}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}^{(Y\scalebox{0.6}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}}
\textstyle{((W\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{Y,W}^{X}\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}id}
\textstyle{((W\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X))\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>((Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}L_{Y,Z}^{X}}
\textstyle{((W\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X))\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y)}

(4)

\forall X,Y\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
L_{X,Y}^{I}\circ(i_{Y}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>id_{X})=id_{(Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}I)}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>i_{X}
;

(5)

\forall X,Y\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the function
\gamma:{\mathbb{C}}(X,Y)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}(I,(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X))
sending
f:X\rightarrow Y
to

(f\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>id_{X})\circ j_{X}
is invertible.

Closed categories are something like monoidal closed categories without tensor products. That is, categories with internal hom functors which are defined directly, not via tensor products and adjunctions.
The structures of closed categories are very similar to the structures of closed multicategories.
As shown in
[
Man12
]
, the category of closed categories are cat-equivalent to the category of closed multicategories with unit objects.

However, when we want to construct (non-symmetric) closed categories as categories of assemblies, it is not sufficient that the applicative structures are
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras, since realizers for
i^{-1}_{X}:X\rightarrow(X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I)
may not exist.
Thus, we add another condition to a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra to realize
i^{-1}_{X}
and obtain the following definition.

{defi}

A
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-
algebra

{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra which contains an element
{\bf I}^{\times}
such that
\forall a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf I}^{\times}a{\bf I}=a
.

Remark 16
.

In
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras, the role we expect to
{\bf I}^{\times}
is to eliminate the “harmless” second argument, which does not necessarily eliminate
{\bf I}
.
Even without specifying
{\bf I}^{\times}
, we can define the same class as
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.
For instance, for a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, suppose there is
{\bf B}^{\times}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf B}^{\times}a{\bf B}=a
.
Then this
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra since
{\lambda}^{\ast}xy.{\bf B}^{\times}x(y{\bf B})
satisfies the axiom of
{\bf I}^{\times}
.
Conversely, for a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, we can take
{\bf B}^{\times}:={\lambda}^{\ast}xy.{\bf I}^{\times}x(y{\bf I}\,{\bf I}\,{\bf I})
and thus
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf B}{\bf I}{\bf B}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras are the same classes.

{exa}

{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
in Example
3.1
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.
Since the planar lambda calculus has the strongly normalizing property, for any closed planar term
M
, there are some
u
and
N
such that
M=_{\beta}\lambda u.N
. Then

\displaystyle(\lambda xyz.x(yz))M(\lambda v.v)

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle\lambda z.M((\lambda v.v)z)

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle\lambda z.(\lambda u.N)z

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle\lambda z.N[z/u]

\displaystyle=_{\alpha}

\displaystyle M

and thus
\lambda xyz.x(yz)
represents
{\bf I}^{\times}
.

Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
(which nicely corresponds to
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras) is also a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, one might suspect that
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras are the same class.
However, these two classes are different ones.
Later in Section
4
, we will discuss an example that separates classes of
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras (Proposition
31
).

The next example based on an ordered group is from
[
Tom21
]
.
(However, here we reverse the direction of the implication symbol
\multimap
of the original example in
[
Tom21
]
.)

{exa}

Take an ordered group
(G,\cdot,e,\leq)
.
Let
T
be a set of elements constructed grammatically as follows:

t::=g\;|\;t\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t^{\prime}\;\;\;(g\in G).

That is,
T
is a set of binary trees whose leaves are labeled by elements of
G
.
We further define a function
|\mathchar 45|:T\rightarrow G
by induction:
|g|:=g
and
|t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}|:=|t_{2}|\cdot|t_{1}|^{-1}
.

Let
|{\mathcal{T}}|
be the powerset of
\{t\in T\mid e\leq|t|\}
.
Then we can get a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{T}}
by
|{\mathcal{T}}|
:

•

For
M,N\in|{\mathcal{T}}|
,
MN:=\{t_{2}\mid\exists t_{1}\in N,(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\in M\}
.

•

{\bf B}:=\{(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})\mid t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in T\}
.
Here

\multimap

joins from the left.

•

{\bf I}:=\{t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid t_{1}\in T\}

•

{\bf I}^{\times}:=\{t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid t_{1},t_{2}\in T\}
.

•

For
M\in|{\mathcal{T}}|
,
{M}^{\bullet}:=\{t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\mid t_{1}\in M,t_{2}\in T\}
.

This example is based on
Comod(\overline{G})
introduced in
[
Has12
]
, which is a category of sets and relations equipped with
G
valued functions.
For any (not necessarily ordered) group
G
,
Comod(\overline{G})
is a pivotal category.

|{\mathcal{T}}|
is a set of maps from the unit object to a reflexive object in (ordered)
Comod(\overline{G})
.
The structure of
{\mathcal{T}}
depends on
G
.
For instance,

\{(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})\mid t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in T\}

acts as the
{\bf C}
-combinator whenever
G
is Abelian.

The above
{\mathcal{T}}
later appears several times as examples of applicative structures of other classes (Example
3.3
,
3.4
,
6.2
).

Proposition 17
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
are closed categories.

Proof 3.3
.

Let
{\mathbb{C}}:=\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.
We give the same bi-functor
(-\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>-):{\mathbb{C}}^{op}\times{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
as in the proof of Proposition
15
.

•

We define the unit object
I
as
(\{\ast\},\|\mathchar 45\|_{I})
, where
\|{\ast}\|_{I}:=\{{\bf I}\}
.

•

j_{X}
is the function sending
\ast
to
id_{X}
, which is realized by
{\bf I}
.

•

i_{X}
is the function sending
(f:\ast\mapsto x)
to
x
, which is realized by
{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}
. The inverse
i_{X}^{-1}
is realized by
{\bf I}^{\times}
.

•

L_{Y,Z}^{X}
is the function sending
g
to the function
(f\mapsto g\circ f)
, which is realized by
{\bf B}
.

•

\gamma
is invertible. Indeed,
\gamma^{-1}
is the function sending
g:I\rightarrow(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)
to the map
g(\ast):X\rightarrow Y
.

It is easy to verify that
j
,
i
and
L
have naturality and satisfy the axioms of the closed category.

For
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
, we can use the same proof for
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.

While we define
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras as a class of total applicative structures, we also can define “partial
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra” naturally.
For a partial
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
,
{\bf I}^{\times}
satisfies that
\forall a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|,{\bf I}^{\times}a{\bf I}\downarrow
and
{\bf I}^{\times}a{\bf I}=a
.
Proposition
17
also holds in the case of partial
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

3.3.
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and monoidal closed categories

In the previous two subsections, we obtain closed multicategories and closed categories as categories of assemblies.
Next we further attempt to obtain a richer categorical structure, the (non-symmetric) tensor products, by categorical realizability.

First, let us consider whether we can realize products by a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra in the same way as PCAs and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras.
Even when we use a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, we can take the object
X\otimes Y
in the same way as PCAs and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras (See the proofs of Proposition
2
and
4
).
That is, for a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, we take an assembly
X\otimes Y
that the underlying set is
|X|\times|Y|
and realizers are

\|{x\otimes y}\|_{X\otimes Y}:=\{{\lambda}^{\ast}t.tpq\mid p\in\|{x}\|_{X},q\in\|{y}\|_{Y}\}.

We also take the unit object in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
in the same way as
{\bf BCI}
-algebras:
|I|:=\{\ast\}
and
\|{\ast}\|_{I}:=\{{\bf I}\}
.
Then is this
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
a monoidal category?
Now let us assume it is.
Take an assembly

A:=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\|\mathchar 45\|_{A})
, where
\|{a}\|_{A}:=\{a\}
.
Then since
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is a monoidal category, the unitor

A\rightarrow I\otimes A
has a realizer
r
, which satisfies that
ra={\lambda}^{\ast}t.t{\bf I}a
.
Taking an elements
C:={\lambda}^{\ast}xyz.rx({\bf B}({\bf B}({\lambda}^{\ast}w.ry({\bf B}w))))z
, this
C
satisfies the axiom of the
{\bf C}
-combinator and make
{\mathcal{A}}
a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.

In summary, when we attempt to make
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
a non-symmetric monoidal category using a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, it follows that
{\mathcal{A}}
is actually a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra and
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
becomes an SMCC.
Therefore, we need some major modification on the definition of realizers of tensor products in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
to make
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
a non-symmetric monoidal category.

One way to solve this problem is supposing a combinator
{\bf P}
expressing the “pairing” operation.
And we define realizers for tensor products as

\|{x\otimes y}\|_{X\otimes Y}:=\{{\bf P}pq\mid p\in\|{x}\|_{X},q\in\|{y}\|_{Y}\}.

Since
{\bf P}pq
itself cannot separate the data of
p
and
q
from
{\bf P}pq
, we need another combinator
{\bf L}
to decompose
{\bf P}pq
.

{defi}

A
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-
algebra

{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra which contains
{\bf L}
and
{\bf P}
such that
\forall x,y,z\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf L}x({\bf P}yz)=xyz
.

A fundamental example of
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras is given as the untyped planar lambda calculus with tensor products.

{exa}

Add the following term construction rules to the planar lambda calculus (Example
3.1
).

\Gamma\vdash M

\Delta\vdash N

(pair construction)

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M\otimes N

\Gamma\vdash M

\Delta,x,y\vdash N

(pair deconstruction)

\Delta,\Gamma\vdash\mbox{{\sf let} ${x\otimes y}$ {\sf be} ${M}$ {\sf in} ${N}$}

We define a relation
\sim
on planar terms as the congruence of the following relations.

•

(\lambda x.M)N\sim M[N/x]

•

M\sim\lambda x.Mx

•

(\mbox{{\sf let} ${x_{1}\otimes x_{2}}$ {\sf be} ${M_{1}\otimes M_{2}}$ {\sf in} ${N}$})\sim N[M_{1}/x_{1}][M_{2}/x_{2}]

•

M\sim(\mbox{{\sf let} ${x\otimes y}$ {\sf be} ${M}$ {\sf in} ${x\otimes y}$})

Let the equational relation
=_{\beta\eta}
be the reflexive, symmetric and transitive closure of
\sim
.
Closed terms modulo
=_{\beta\eta}
form a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, which we call
{\mathcal{L}}_{\otimes}
in this paper.
Here
\lambda xyz.x(yz)
,
\lambda tu.(\mbox{{\sf let} ${x\otimes y}$ {\sf be} ${u}$ {\sf in} ${txy}$})
and
\lambda xy.(x\otimes y)
are the representatives of
{\bf I}^{\times}
,
{\bf L}
and
{\bf P}
respectively.

Unlike the planar lambda calculus of Example
3.2
(that does not have tensor products) does not need the
\eta
-equality to be a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, the planar lambda calculus with tensor products of Example
3.3
needs the
\beta\eta
-equality to use
\lambda xyz.x(yz)
as
{\bf I}^{\times}
.
Indeed,
(\lambda xyz.x(yz))((\lambda u.u)\otimes(\lambda v.v))(\lambda w.w)
is
\beta\eta
-equal to
(\lambda u.u)\otimes(\lambda v.v)
but not
\beta
-equal to it.

Remark 18
.

When constructing linear lambda terms with tensor products, we often suppose a constant
\star
for the unit (cf.
[
Abr93
]
). For the above example, we can add the following rules to the term construction rules.

(star introduction)

\vdash\star

\vdash M

\Gamma\vdash N

(star elimination)

\Gamma\vdash\mbox{{\sf let} ${\star}$ {\sf be} ${M}$ {\sf in} ${N}$}

However, for our aim that constructing monoidal categories by categorical realizability, this
\star
is not needed since we can use
{\bf I}
as the realizer of the unit instead of
\star
.

{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras correspond to the lambda calculus with tensor products, which has components other than applications, unlike the ordinary/linear/planar lambda calculus.
Thus, we cannot state the combinatory completeness property for
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras in the same way we have seen in previous sections.
Here we only show the special case of the combinatory completeness property for
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

Proposition 19
.

Any closed term
M
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{\otimes}
is
\beta\eta
-equivalent to some term
\langle\![M]\!\rangle
that is constructed from
{\bf B}:=\lambda xyz.x(yz)
,
{\bf I}:=\lambda x.x
,
{\bf L}:=\lambda tu.(\mbox{{\sf let} ${x\otimes y}$ {\sf be} ${u}$ {\sf in} ${txy}$})
and
{\bf P}:=\lambda xy.x\otimes y
using the application and the unary operation
{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}:M\mapsto\lambda x.xM
.

Proof 3.4
.

We inductively define the function
\langle\![\mathchar 45]\!\rangle
.

•

\langle\![x]\!\rangle:=x

•

\langle\![MN]\!\rangle:=\langle\![M]\!\rangle\langle\![N]\!\rangle

•

\langle\![M\otimes N]\!\rangle:={\bf P}\langle\![M]\!\rangle\langle\![N]\!\rangle

•

\langle\![\mbox{{\sf let} ${x\otimes y}$ {\sf be} ${M}$ {\sf in} ${N}$}]\!\rangle:={\bf L}\langle\![\lambda xy.N]\!\rangle\langle\![M]\!\rangle

•

\langle\![\lambda xy.M]\!\rangle:=\langle\![\lambda x.\langle\![\lambda y.M]\!\rangle]\!\rangle

•

\langle\![\lambda x.x]\!\rangle:={\bf I}

•

\langle\![\lambda x.MN]\!\rangle:=\begin{cases}{\bf B}{\langle\![N]\!\rangle}^{\bullet}\langle\![\lambda x.M]\!\rangle&(x\in FV(M))\\
{\bf B}\langle\![M]\!\rangle\langle\![\lambda x.N]\!\rangle&(x\in FV(N))\end{cases}

•

\langle\![\lambda x.M\otimes N]\!\rangle:=\begin{cases}{\bf B}{\langle\![N]\!\rangle}^{\bullet}({\bf B}{\bf P}\langle\![\lambda x.M]\!\rangle)&(x\in FV(M))\\
{\bf B}({\bf P}\langle\![M]\!\rangle)\langle\![\lambda x.N]\!\rangle&(x\in FV(N))\end{cases}

•

\langle\![\lambda x.(\mbox{{\sf let} ${y\otimes z}$ {\sf be} ${M}$ {\sf in} ${N}$})]\!\rangle:=\begin{cases}{\bf B}({\bf L}\langle\![\lambda yz.N]\!\rangle)\langle\![\lambda x.M]\!\rangle&(x\in FV(M))\\
{\bf B}{\langle\![M]\!\rangle}^{\bullet}({\bf B}{\bf L}\langle\![\lambda xyz.N]\!\rangle)&(x\in FV(N))\end{cases}

It is easy to see that
M=_{\beta\eta}\langle\![M]\!\rangle
for any closed term
M
.

Next we give an example of
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra similar to Example
3.2
.

{exa}

Take an ordered group
(G,\cdot,e,\leq)
.
Let
T^{\prime}
be a set whose elements are constructed grammatically as follows:

t::=g\;|\;t\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t^{\prime}\;|\;t\otimes t^{\prime}\;\;\;(g\in G).

That is,
T^{\prime}
is a set of binary trees whose leaves are labeled by elements of
G
, and whose nodes are two colored by

\multimap

and
\otimes
.
We further define a function
|\mathchar 45|:T^{\prime}\rightarrow G
by induction:
|g|:=g
,
|t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}|:=|t_{2}|\cdot|t_{1}|^{-1}
and
|t_{1}\otimes t_{2}|:=|t_{1}|\cdot|t_{2}|
.

Let
|{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}|
be the powerset of
\{t\in T^{\prime}\mid e\leq|t|\}
.
Then we can get a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
by
|{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}|
:

•

For
M,N\in|{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}|
,
MN:=\{t_{2}\mid\exists t_{1}\in N,(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\in M\}
.

•

{\bf B}:=\{(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})\mid t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in T^{\prime}\}
.

•

{\bf I}:=\{t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid t_{1}\in T^{\prime}\}
.

•

{\bf I}^{\times}:=\{t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid t_{1},t_{2}\in T^{\prime}\}
.

•

{\bf L}:=\{t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{1}\otimes t_{2})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\mid t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in T^{\prime}\}
.

•

{\bf P}:=\{(t_{1}\otimes t_{2})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid t_{1},t_{2}\in T^{\prime}\}
.

•

For
M\in|{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}|
,
{M}^{\bullet}:=\{t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\mid t_{1}\in M,t_{2}\in T^{\prime}\}
.

In the above example, we prepare
\otimes
in the construction of
T^{\prime}
to express
{\bf L}
and
{\bf P}
.
However, in fact,
{\mathcal{T}}
in Example
3.2
is already a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra even without
\otimes
.

{exa}

In
{\mathcal{T}}
of Example
3.2
, we have
{\bf P}
-combinator and
{\bf L}
-combinator as

•

{\bf P}:=\{t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(e\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid t_{1},t_{2}\in T\}
;

•

{\bf L}:=\{t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(e\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2}))\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\mid t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in T\}
.

This is a less standard example in that
{\mathcal{T}}
uses
t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(e\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})
as the role of
t_{1}\otimes t_{2}
.

For another example, as well as we can construct an LCA (and the based
{\bf BCI}
-algebra) from a “reflexive object” (See
[
AHS02
]
and
[
Hag00
]
), we can get
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras by appropriate settings.

{exa}

Let
({\mathbb{C}},\otimes,I)
be a monoidal closed category and

\Phi:{\mathbb{C}}(-\otimes X,-)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}(-,-\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)
be the adjunction.
Suppose an object
V
that has:

(1)

an isomorphism
r:(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\rightarrow V
and
s:=r^{-1}
;

(2)

a
retraction

t:(V\otimes V)\triangleleft V:u
, that is, maps
t:V\otimes V\rightarrow V
and
u:V\rightarrow V\otimes V
such that
u\circ t=id_{V\otimes V}
.

Then the set of maps
{\mathbb{C}}(I,V)
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.

•

For maps
M,N:I\rightarrow V
, the application is defined as

I\xrightarrow{{\rm unitor}}I\otimes I\xrightarrow{(s\circ M)\otimes N}(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\otimes V\xrightarrow{ev}V.

•

Take a map
f:(V\otimes V)\otimes V\rightarrow V
as

(V\otimes V)\otimes V\xrightarrow{{\rm associator}}V\otimes(V\otimes V)\xrightarrow{s\otimes(ev\circ(s\otimes id))}(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\otimes V\xrightarrow{ev}V.

The
{\bf B}
-combinator is given as
r\circ\Phi(r\circ\Phi(r\circ\Phi(f))\circ\lambda_{V})
, where
\lambda_{V}:I\otimes V\rightarrow V
is the unitor.

•

The
{\bf I}
-combinator is
r\circ\Phi(\lambda_{V})
.

•

The
{\bf B}
-combinator given above satisfies the axiom of the
{\bf I}^{\times}
-combinator.
Here we use

r\circ s=id_{V}
, and thus we need to assume
r
is an isomorphism (not merely a retraction).

•

Take a map
g:V\otimes V\rightarrow V
as

V\otimes V\xrightarrow{s\otimes u}(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\otimes(V\otimes V)\xrightarrow{ev\circ({\rm associator})}V\otimes V\xrightarrow{ev\circ(s\otimes id)}V.

The
{\bf L}
-combinator is
r\circ\Phi(r\circ\Phi(g)\circ\lambda_{V})
.

•

The
{\bf P}
-combinator is
r\circ\Phi(r\circ\Phi(t)\circ\lambda_{V})
.

•

Given arbitrary
M:I\rightarrow V
,
{M}^{\bullet}
is
r\circ\Phi(ev\circ(s\otimes M)\circ\rho_{V}\circ\lambda_{V})
. Here
\rho_{V}:V\rightarrow V\otimes I
is the unitor.

We will use the above
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra later in the last of Section
6.1
.

Next we show that
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras induce monoidal closed categories.

Proposition 20
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is a monoidal closed category.

Proof 3.5
.

Since
{\mathcal{A}}
is also a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, we can use the combinatory completeness for the planar lambda calculus.

•

For objects
X
and
Y
, the underlying set of
X\otimes Y
is
|X|\times|Y|
. Realizers are defined as
