\|{x\otimes y}\|_{X\otimes Y}:=\{{\bf P}pq\mid p\in\|{x}\|_{X},q\in\|{y}\|_{Y}\}.

•

For
f:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
, the map
f\otimes g
is the function sending
x\otimes y
to
f(x)\otimes g(y)
.
A realizer for
f\otimes g
is
{\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}pq.{\bf P}(r_{f}p)(r_{g}q))
.

•

The underlying set of the unit object
I
is a singleton
\{\ast\}
. The realizer is
\|{\ast}\|_{I}:=\{{\bf I}\}
.

•

The left unitor
\lambda_{X}:I\otimes X\rightarrow X
sends
\ast\otimes x
to
x
, whose realizer is
{\bf L}{\bf I}
.
A realizer of
\lambda_{X}^{-1}
is
{\bf P}{\bf I}
.

•

The right unitor
\rho_{X}:X\rightarrow X\otimes I
sends
x
to
x\otimes\ast
, whose realizer is
{\lambda}^{\ast}p.{\bf P}p{\bf I}
.
A realizer of
\rho_{X}^{-1}
is
{\bf L}{\bf I}^{\times}
.

•

The associator
\alpha_{XYZ}:(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\rightarrow X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)
sends
(x\otimes y)\otimes z
to
x\otimes(y\otimes z)
.
A realizer of
\alpha_{XYZ}
is
{\bf L}({\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}pqr.{\bf P}p({\bf P}qr)))
.
A realizer of
\alpha_{XYZ}^{-1}
is
{\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}pu.{\bf L}(Mp)u)
, where
M:={\lambda}^{\ast}pqr.{\bf P}({\bf P}pq)r
.

•

For objects
X
and
Y
, the underlying set of
Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X
is
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})}(X,Y)
. Realizers are defined as

\|{f}\|_{Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X}:=\{r\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}
.

•

For
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
,
g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f
is the function sending a map
h:X\rightarrow Y
to
g\circ h\circ f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
.
A realizer for
g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}uv.r_{g}(u(r_{f}v))
.

•

The evaluation map
ev:(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\otimes X\rightarrow Y
sends
f\otimes x
to
f(x)
, which is realized by
{\bf L}{\bf I}
.

•

For any map
f:Z\otimes X\rightarrow Y
, there exists a unique map
g:Z\rightarrow(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)
which satisfies

ev\circ(g\otimes id_{X})=f
. This
g
is given as the function sending
z
to the function
x\mapsto f(z\otimes x)
, which is realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}rp.r_{f}({\bf P}rp)
.

Similar to the case of
{\bf BCI}
-algebras (Proposition
4
and
5
), we cannot use the same proof of Proposition
20
to the case of
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
.
We prove that
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
on a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is a monoidal closed category by the same modification used in the proof of Proposition
5
.
That is, we take the inclusion functor
G:\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})\hookrightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and the left adjoint
F:\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
, and define the tensor product
\boxtimes
in
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
as
X\boxtimes Y:=F(GX\otimes GY)
.

Proposition 21
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra,
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
is a monoidal closed category. ∎

For functors given by applicative morphisms between
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras, the next properties hold.

Proposition 22
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
be
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
is an applicative morphism. Then
{\gamma}_{\ast}:\sf{Asm}({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({{\mathcal{A}}_{2}})
is a lax monoidal functor.

Proof 3.6
.

A realizer for
I_{2}\rightarrow{\gamma}_{\ast}(I_{1})
is in the set
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.u(\gamma({\bf I}_{1}))
.

A realizer for
({\gamma}_{\ast}X)\otimes_{2}({\gamma}_{\ast}Y)\rightarrow{\gamma}_{\ast}(X\otimes_{1}Y)
is in
{\bf L}_{2}({\lambda}^{\ast}pq.r_{\gamma}(r_{\gamma}(\gamma{\bf P}_{1})p)q)
.

Proposition 23
.

For
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
and an adjoint pair

(\delta\dashv\gamma):{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
, the adjunction
({\delta}_{\ast}\dashv{\gamma}_{\ast}):\sf{Asm}({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({{\mathcal{A}}_{2}})
is monoidal.

Proof 3.7
.

We show that the left adjoint
{\delta}_{\ast}
is strong monoidal.
Since
{\delta}_{\ast}
is lax monoidal by the previous proposition, it is sufficient to show that there are realizers for maps
{\delta}_{\ast}I_{2}\rightarrow I_{1}
and
{\delta}_{\ast}(X\otimes_{2}Y)\rightarrow{\delta}_{\ast}X\otimes_{1}{\delta}_{\ast}Y
.
A realizer for the former is

{\lambda}^{\ast}x.{\bf e}(r_{\delta}(\delta({\lambda}^{\ast}y.y(\gamma{\bf I}_{1})))x)
.
A realizer for the latter is

{\lambda}^{\ast}z.{\bf e}(r_{\delta}(\delta({\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}uv.r_{\gamma}(r_{\gamma}(\gamma{\bf P})({\bf i}u))({\bf i}v))))z)
.
Here
{\bf e}\in|{\mathcal{A}}_{1}|
is an element such that
\forall x\in|{\mathcal{A}}_{1}|,{\bf e}(\delta(\gamma x))=x
and
{\bf i}\in|{\mathcal{A}}_{2}|
is an element such that
\forall y\in|{\mathcal{A}}_{2}|,{\bf i}y=\gamma(\delta y)
, that are obtained by the assumption that
\gamma
and
\delta
form an adjoint pair.

3.4.
Bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras and monoidal bi-closed categories

Let us consider once again why non-symmetric tensor products in categories of assemblies cannot be constructed from
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras,
from the viewpoint of the “polymorphic encoding.”
In the second-order linear logic, a tensor product
X\otimes Y
can be interpreted as

\forall\alpha.(X\multimap Y\multimap\alpha)\multimap\alpha
. (This interpretation is seen in
[
BMP06
]
, for instance.)
This formula
(X\multimap Y\multimap\alpha)\multimap\alpha
corresponds to the type inhabited by
\lambda t.txy
in the typed linear lambda calculus.
This correspondence connected to that (in a PCA or a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra,) a realizer of
x\otimes y\in|X\otimes Y|
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}t.tpq
for
p\in\|{x}\|_{X}
and
q\in\|{y}\|_{Y}
.
What matters here is that the interpretation
X\otimes Y\cong\forall\alpha.(X\multimap Y\multimap\alpha)\multimap\alpha
holds only when the tensor product is symmetric.
Whereas, for the non-symmetric cases,
X\otimes Y
is expressed as
\forall\alpha.(\alpha\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\multimap\alpha
or
\forall\alpha.\alpha\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Y\multimap X\multimap\alpha)
.
Here we need to distinguish two sorts of implications

\multimap

and
\multimap
.
In an applicative structure like a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, we cannot distinguish them since we only have one sort of application.

Conversely, providing some structure in an applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}
that allows to distinguish these two implications, we may be able to construct non-symmetric tensor products in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.
From this viewpoint, we introduced bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras in
[
Tom22
]
.

In this subsection, we recall bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras from
[
Tom22
]
.
First we recall a variant of the lambda calculus, which is an example of an applicative structure with two sorts of applications.

{defi}

Bi-planar lambda terms
are constructed by the following rules:

(identity)

x\vdash x

\Gamma,x\vdash M

(right abstraction)

\Gamma\vdash({M}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{$\mapsto$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x})

x,\Gamma\vdash M

(left abstraction)

\Gamma\vdash({x}\hskip 1.0pt{\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{M})

\Gamma\vdash M

\Delta\vdash N

(right application)

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M\reflectbox{{$\vec{@}$}}N

\Delta\vdash N

\Gamma\vdash M

(left application)

\Delta,\Gamma\vdash N{\vec{@}}M

Note that here is none of weakening, contraction nor exchange rules.

For the sake of clarity, we will classify right and left by red and blue color.
That is, we write each of them as
M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N
,
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{M}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\mapsto$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
,
N\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}M
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{M}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
.

We define a relation
\rightarrow_{\beta}
on bi-planar lambda terms as the congruence of the following relations:

•

(right
\beta
-reduction)
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{M}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\mapsto$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N\rightarrow_{\beta}M[N/x]

•

(left
\beta
-reduction)
N\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{M}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}\rightarrow_{\beta}M[N/x]

The
bi-planar lambda calculus
consists of bi-planar lambda terms and the reflexive, symmetric and transitive closure of
\rightarrow_{\beta}
as the equational relation
=_{\beta}
.

Basic properties about the
\beta
-reduction
\rightarrow_{\beta}
, such as the confluence and the strongly normalizing property, can be shown in the same way as the proof for the linear lambda calculus.

Remark 24
.

The bi-planar lambda calculus is not essentially a new concept, since it often appears as the Curry-Howard corresponding calculus with the Lambek calculus (cf.
[
JIdP18
]
).
However, note that unlike the calculus corresponding to the Lambek calculus, the bi-planar lambda calculus is based on untyped setting.
The reason why we use a less-standard notation is to shorten the length of terms and to make them easier to read.

Then we define a class of applicative structures which we call bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras.

{defi}

A total applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}})
is a
bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra
iff there is an additional total binary operation

\vec{@}

on
|{\mathcal{A}}|
and
|{\mathcal{A}}|
contains several special elements:

(1)

\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}$}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x,y,z\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
((\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x)\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y)\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}z=x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}z)
.

(2)

\vec{\bf B}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x,y,z\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
z\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}\vec{\bf B}))=(z\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y)\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}x
.

(3)

\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf D$}}$}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x,y,z\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}((\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf D$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y)\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}z)=(x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y)\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}z
.

(4)

\vec{\bf D}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x,y,z\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
(z\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}\vec{\bf D}))\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x=z\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x)
.

(5)

\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x=x
.

(6)

\vec{\bf I}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}\vec{\bf I}=x
.

(7)

For each
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{a}^{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}\triangleleft}}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
({a}^{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}\triangleleft}})\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x=x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}a
.

(8)

For each
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{a}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}({a}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})=a\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x
.

We call

\vec{@}

and

\vec{@}

as
right application
and
left application
respectively.
We often write

{\mathcal{A}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}},\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}})
for a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}})
with the left application

\vec{@}

.

In the sequel, we use

\vec{@}

as a left-associative operation and often omit unnecessary parentheses, while we do not omit parentheses for

\vec{@}

.
For instance,
(u\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}v\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}w)\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}((x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y)\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}z)
denotes
((u\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}v)\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}w)\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}((x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y)\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}z)
.

The definition of bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras is intended having a good correspondence with the bi-planar lambda calculus.

{exa}

Untyped closed bi-planar lambda terms modulo
=_{\beta}
form a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra, which we call
{\mathcal{L}}_{B}
in this paper.
We give a few examples of representatives:
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}z)}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\mapsto$}}\hskip 1.0pt{z}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\mapsto$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\mapsto$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
represents

\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}

;
{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{y}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}z)}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\mapsto$}}\hskip 1.0pt{z}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
represents
\vec{\bf D}
;
{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
represents
{M}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
.

Proposition 25

(combinatory completeness for bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras)
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}},\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}})
be a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.
A polynomial over
{\mathcal{A}}
is defined as a syntactic expression generated by variables, elements of
|{\mathcal{A}}|
and the applications

\vec{@}

and

\vec{@}

.
For a polynomial
M
over
{\mathcal{A}}
and the rightmost variable
x
of
M
, if
x
appears exactly once in
M
, there exists a polynomial
M^{\prime}
such that the free variables of
M^{\prime}
are the free variables of
M
excluding
x
and
M^{\prime}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}a=M[a/x]
for all
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
. We write such
M^{\prime}
as
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{M}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.
Also, for a polynomial
N
over
{\mathcal{A}}
and the leftmost variable
y
of
N
, if
y
appears exactly once in
N
, there exists a polynomial
N^{\prime}
such that the free variables of
N^{\prime}
are the free variables of
N
excluding
y
and
a\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}N^{\prime}=N[a/y]
for all
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
. We write such
N^{\prime}
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{y}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
.

Proof 3.8
.

We define
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{M}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
by induction on the structure of
M
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}:=\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}:=\begin{cases}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{(\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf D$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\vec{\bf I}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N)}^{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}\triangleleft}}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{M}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}&(x\in FV(M))\\
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{N}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}&(x\in FV(N))\end{cases}

Note that in case
x\in FV(M)
,
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf D$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\vec{\bf I}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N
has no variables since
x
is the rightmost free variable in
M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{N\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}M}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}:=\begin{cases}N\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}({\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{M}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}\vec{\bf D})&(x\in FV(M))\\
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}({M}^{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}\triangleleft}})\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{N}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}&(x\in FV(N))\end{cases}

Note that in case
x\in FV(N)
,
M
has no variables since
x
is the rightmost free variable in
N\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}M
.

The case of the left abstraction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{y}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
is given in the same way, with all the left and right constructs reversed.

Next we give another example of bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra which is introduced in
[
Tom22
]
and similar to Example
3.3
.

{exa}

Take an ordered group
(G,\cdot,e,\leq)
.
Let
T^{\prime\prime}
be a set whose elements are constructed grammatically as follows:

t::=g\;|\;t\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t^{\prime}\;|\;t\multimap t^{\prime}\;\;\;(g\in G).

That is,
T^{\prime\prime}
is a set of binary trees whose leaves are labeled by elements of
G
, and whose nodes are two colored by

\multimap

and
\multimap
.
We further define a function
|\mathchar 45|:T^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow G
by induction:
|g|:=g
,
|t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}|:=|t_{2}|\cdot|t_{1}|^{-1}
and
|t_{1}\multimap t_{2}|:=|t_{1}|^{-1}\cdot|t_{2}|
.

Let
|{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime\prime}|
be the powerset of
\{t\in T^{\prime\prime}\mid e\leq|t|\}
.
Then we can get a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime\prime}
by
|{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime\prime}|
:

•

For
M,N\in|{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime\prime}|
,
M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N:=\{t_{2}\mid\exists t_{1}\in N,(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\in M\}
.

•

For
M,N\in|{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime\prime}|
,
N\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}M:=\{t_{2}\mid\exists t_{1}\in N,(t_{1}\multimap t_{2})\in M\}
.

•

\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}$}:=\{(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})\mid t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in T^{\prime\prime}\}
, dual for
\vec{\bf B}
.

•

\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf D$}}$}:=\{((t_{1}\multimap t_{2})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{3})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{1}\multimap(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{3}))\mid t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in T^{\prime\prime}\}
, dual for
\vec{\bf D}
.

•

\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}:=\{t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid t_{1}\in T^{\prime\prime}\}
, dual for
\vec{\bf I}
.

•

For
M\in|{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime\prime}|
,
{M}^{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}\triangleleft}}:=\{t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid(t_{1}\multimap t_{2})\in M\}
, dual for
{M}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
.

In the above example, we prepare
\multimap
in the construction of
T^{\prime\prime}
to express the left application.
However, in fact,
{\mathcal{T}}
in Example
3.2
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra even without preparing
\multimap
.

{exa}

Let
T
be the same set in Example
3.2
.
For
t,t^{\prime}\in T
, we define
t\multimap t^{\prime}\in T
as
(e\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(e\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t^{\prime})
.
Then
{\mathcal{T}}
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra, whose components are taken in the same way as Example
3.4
.

Next we give some basic properties of bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras.

Proposition 26
.

(1)

Any bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra is also a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.

(2)

Any
{\bf BCI}
-algebra is also a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra whose left and right applications coincide.

(3)

When
{\mathcal{A}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}})
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra, the left application is unique up to isomorphism. That is, when both
(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}},\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}_{1})
and
(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}},\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}_{2})
are bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras,
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}_{1})
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}_{2})
are isomorphic as applicative structures, where
x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}_{i}y:=y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}_{i}x
.

(4)

Let
{\mathcal{A}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}},\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}})
be a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra and take an applicative structure

{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}:=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}^{\prime})
by
x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}^{\prime}y:=y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}x
.
Then
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra iff
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.
Moreover, in such a case,
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
are isomorphic as applicative structures.

Proof 3.9
.

(1)

{\bf B}
,
{\bf I}
,
{\bf I}^{\times}
,
{\bf L}
,
{\bf P}
and
{a}^{\bullet}
are given as

\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}

,

\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}

,
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\vec{\bf I})}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{t}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{t\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}a}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}\triangleleft}}
respectively.

(2)

For a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}})
,
(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}},\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}})
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra when we take
y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}x:=x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y
. Here
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}$}=\vec{\bf B}:={\bf B}
,
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf D$}}$}=\vec{\bf D}:={\bf C}
,
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}=\vec{\bf I}:={\bf I}
and
{a}^{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}\triangleleft}}={a}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}:=a
.

(3)

By the combinatory completeness of
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
, we have
L:={\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}_{2}x}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
such that
L\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x=y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}_{2}x=x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}_{2}y
.
By the combinatory completeness of
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
, we have an element

r:={\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{y}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{L\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
, which satisfies
r\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}_{1}x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}_{1}y=L\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x=x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}_{2}y
.
This
r
realizes the applicative morphism
i_{1}:{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
given as the identity function on
|{\mathcal{A}}|
.
Similarly we have the inverse applicative morphism
i_{2}:{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
given as the identity function.

i_{1}
and
i_{2}
are the isomorphisms between
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
.

(4)

Suppose that
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra, that is, there is some element
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf C$}}$}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that

\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf C$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}z=x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}z\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y
.
Take an element
\vec{\bf C}:={\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{y}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{z}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf C$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}z\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
, where
M:={\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(z\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}x)}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{z}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.

\vec{\bf B}
,
\vec{\bf I}
and
\vec{\bf C}
make
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.
Similarly, when we suppose
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra,
{\mathcal{A}}
is also a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.

Furthermore, when we suppose
{\mathcal{A}}
(and also
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
) is a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra, we have an element

r:=\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf C$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}x}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
, which realizes the applicative morphism
i:{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
given as the identity function.
Similarly we have the inverse applicative morphism
i^{\prime}:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
given as the identity function, and thus
{\mathcal{A}}\cong{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
.

By (
1
) and (
2
) of the above proposition, the class of bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras is the class of applicative structures in between
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras.
We named the “
{\bf L}
-combinator” of
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras by the reason that it is represented as
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
in a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra, that gives the “left” application of two arguments.

Remark 27
.

Although
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
always acts as a
{\bf L}
-combinator in a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra, it is not the only way to take a
{\bf L}
-combinator.
Indeed, in Example
3.4
,
{\mathcal{T}}
has a
{\bf L}
-combinator as

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{(\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}\vec{\bf D})}^{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}\triangleleft}}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>((e\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(e\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2}))\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid t_{1},t_{2}\in T\}\mbox{,}

which is different from the
{\bf L}
-combinator taken in Example
3.3
.

Since a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is also a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, we know that
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) is a monoidal closed category.
Moreover, we can show that the categories of assemblies on bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras are not just a monoidal closed categories, but are monoidal bi-closed categories, having richer categorical structures.
A
monoidal bi-closed category
is a monoidal category
{\mathbb{C}}
with two sorts of adjunction
{\mathbb{C}}(X\otimes Y,Z)\cong{\mathbb{C}}(X,Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y)
and
{\mathbb{C}}(X\otimes Y,Z)\cong{\mathbb{C}}(Y,X\multimap Z)
.

Proposition 28
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}})
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is a monoidal bi-closed category.

Proof 3.10
.

Let

\vec{@}

be the left application of
{\mathcal{A}}
.

•

A realizer for identities is

\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}

.

•

A realizer for the composition of
f:X\rightarrow Y
and
g:Y\rightarrow Z
is
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}r_{g}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}r_{f}
.

•

For objects
X
and
Y
, the underlying set of
X\otimes Y
is
|X|\times|Y|
. Realizers are defined as

\|{x\otimes y}\|:=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{t}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{t\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}p\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}q}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}\mid p\in\|{x}\|_{X},q\in\|{y}\|_{Y}\}.

•

For
f:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
, the map
f\otimes g
is the function sending
x\otimes y
to
f(x)\otimes g(y)
.
A realizer for
f\otimes g
is
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{t}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{t\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}(r_{f}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}p)\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}(r_{g}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}q)}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{q}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{p}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}u}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{u}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.

•

The underlying set of the unit object

is a singleton
\{\ast\}
. The realizer is

.

to

, whose realizer is

is
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{t}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{t\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}p}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{p}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.

{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{N}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{r}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{q}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}v}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{v}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{p}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}u}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{u}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}

.

•

For objects
X
and
Y
, the underlying set of

is
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})}(X,Y)
. Realizers are

\|{f}\|_{Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X}:=\{r\mid\mbox{$r$ is a realizer of $f$}\}.

•

For

and

,

is the function sending a map

to the map

.
A realizer for

is
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{r_{g}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}(u\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}(r_{f}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}v))}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{v}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{u}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.

to

, which is realized by

.

•

For any map
f:Z\otimes X\rightarrow Y
, there exists a unique map

which satisfies

ev\circ(g\otimes id_{X})=f
. This

is given as the function sending
z
to the function

, which is realized by
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{r_{f}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{t}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{t\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}q\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}p}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{p}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{q}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.

•

For objects
X
and
Y
, the underlying set of
X\multimap Y
is is
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})}(X,Y)
. Realizers are

\|{f}\|_{X\multimap Y}:=\{r\mid\mbox{$a\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}r\in\|{f(x)}\|_{Y}$ for any $x\in|X|$ and $a\in\|{x}\|_{X}$}\}.

This set is not empty since
{(r_{f})}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
is in the set for a realizer
r_{f}
of
f
.

•

For
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
,
f\multimap g
is the function sending a map
h:X\rightarrow Y
to the map
g\circ h\circ f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
. A realizer for
f\multimap g
is
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{v}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{r_{g}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}((r_{f}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}v)\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}u)}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{u}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.

•

The evaluation map
ev^{\prime}:X\otimes(X\multimap Y)\rightarrow Y
sends
x\otimes f
to
f(x)
, which is realized by
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{p\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}v}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{v}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{p}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}u}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{u}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.

•

For any map
f:X\otimes Z\rightarrow Y
, there exists a unique map
g:Z\rightarrow(X\multimap Y)
which satisfies

ev^{\prime}\circ(id_{X}\otimes g)=f
. This
g
is given as the function sending
z
to the function
x\mapsto f(x\otimes z)
, which is realized by
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{p}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{r_{f}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{t}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{t\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}p\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}q}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{q}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.

For the category of modest sets, we use the same discussion as Proposition
5
.
That is, for the functor
F
that is left adjoint of the inclusion functor
G:\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
, we define tensor products
\boxtimes
in
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
as
X\boxtimes Y:=F(GX\otimes GY)
.

Proposition 29
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}})
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra,
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
is a monoidal bi-closed category. ∎

In Proposition
28
,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is the category of assemblies on the applicative structure
(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}})
.
Even if we employ the left application

\vec{@}

to construct the category of assemblies, we can obtain a category with the same structures as
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
, as the next proposition says.

Proposition 30
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}},\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}})
be a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.
When we take an applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}^{\prime})
by
x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}^{\prime}y:=y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}x
,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
are isomorphic as categories.
Moreover,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is monoidally isomorphic to
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
with the reversed tensor products.
That is, there is an isomorphism
R:\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
such that
R(I)\cong I^{\prime}
,
R^{-1}(I^{\prime})\cong I
,

R(X\otimes Y)\cong RY\otimes^{\prime}RX
and
R^{-1}(X^{\prime}\otimes^{\prime}Y^{\prime})\cong R^{-1}Y^{\prime}\otimes R^{-1}X^{\prime}
hold.

Proof 3.11
.

For a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
, the map is also a map in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
since the realizer exists as
{r_{f}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
.
Therefore, we can take a functor
R:\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
which sends objects to the same objects and maps to the same maps.
Similarly we can get
R^{-1}
which sends objects to the same objects and maps to the same maps.

{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra by taking the left application
x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}^{\prime}y:=y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x
.
We define the monoidal structure
(\otimes^{\prime},I^{\prime})
on
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
in the same way as Proposition
28
.
Here the realizers for tensor products are
\|{x\otimes^{\prime}y}\|_{X\otimes^{\prime}Y}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{q\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(p\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}t)}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{t}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}\mid p\in\|{x}\|_{X},q\in\|{y}\|_{Y}\}
.
A realizer for
R(I)\rightarrow I^{\prime}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{u}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{u\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\vec{\bf I}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
and a realizer for the inverse is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{u}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}u}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
.
A realizer for
R(X\otimes Y)\rightarrow RY\otimes^{\prime}RX
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{u}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{p\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(q\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}t)}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{t}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{q}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{p}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}u}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
and a realizer for the inverse is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{u}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{u\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{q}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{p}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{t}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{t\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}p\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}q}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
.

Similar for the realizers related to
R^{-1}
.

We can define “partial bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras” naturally.
Similar to partial
{\bf BCI}
-algebras discussed in Remark
6
, for a partial bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
:

•

\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is not generally a monoidal bi-closed category;

•

adding an extra element
\bot
,
{\mathcal{A}}
naturally extends to a total bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\bot}
;

•

\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is the full subcategory of
\sf{Asm}({{\mathcal{A}}_{\bot}})
.

Here the
\bot
does not need to be two (for

\vec{@}

and for

\vec{@}

), just one.

4.
Separation of classes of applicative strctures

As we have already mentioned, the classes of applicative structures in this paper form a hierarchy summarized in the following table (Table
3
).
However, we have not yet shown the strictness of the hierarchy.
To show the strictness of the each inclusion, it is sufficient to provide an applicative structure separating the classes, that is, an applicative structure belonging to one side of the class but not belonging to the other.
In this section we give several such applicative structures, as summarized in Table
3
.

Table 3.
Separation

Applicative structure

Definition

It is a…

It is not a…

Proposition

{\mathcal{L}}_{Pc}

Example
4.1

{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra

{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra

31

{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{Pc}

Example
4.1

{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra

{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra

32

{\mathcal{C}}

-algebra

Inclusions

{\bf BCI}
-algebras
\subsetneq

{\bf BDI}

{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
