4.1.
Proofs of separations

First we show that the planar lambda calculus with a constant separates
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

{exa}

Suppose a constant symbol
c
and add the following constant rule to the construction rules of planar lambda terms (See Example
2.2
and
3.1
).

(constant)

\vdash c

We assume no additional reduction rules about the constant.
That is, for instance,
c(\lambda x.x)c
has no redex.
Closed planar terms (which may contain
c
) modulo
=_{\beta}
form a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, which we call
{\mathcal{L}}_{Pc}
.

Even adding the constant
c
, the planar lambda calculus still has the properties of confluence and strongly normalizing.

Proposition 31
.

{\mathcal{L}}_{Pc}
is a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra but not a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.
Hence,

{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras
\subsetneq

{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

Proof 4.1
.

Assume that
{\mathcal{L}}_{Pc}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.
That is, assume there exist terms
I
and
I^{\times}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{Pc}
such that
IM=_{\beta}M
and
I^{\times}MI=_{\beta}M
for any term
M
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{Pc}
.
We take
I
and
I^{\times}
as
\beta
-normal terms w.l.o.g.
If
M=_{\beta}N
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{Pc}
, the number of appearance of
c
is equal between
M
and
N
.
Thus, since
I^{\times}cI=_{\beta}c
,
I
and
I^{\times}
cannot contain
c
.

•

When
I^{\times}c
is
\beta
-normal,
I^{\times}cI
is also
\beta
-normal and obviously not equal to
c
.
This contradicts to the confluence of the planar lambda calculus (with constant
c
).

•

When
I^{\times}=\lambda u.J
for some
J
and
u
,
I^{\times}cI=_{\beta}(J[c/u])I
.

–

When
J=\lambda v.J^{\prime}
for some
J^{\prime}
and
v
,
I^{\times}cI=_{\beta}J^{\prime}[c/u][I/v]
.
Suppose
v
receives just
n
arguments
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}

(n\geq 0)
in
J^{\prime}
.

J^{\prime}=C[vN_{1}\dots N_{n}]
for some context
C[-]
which contains
u
to the left of the hole
[-]
.
For the
\beta
-normal form
N
of
IN_{1}\dots N_{n}
,
I^{\times}cI=_{\beta}(C[N])[c/u]
.

(C[N])[c/u]
is
\beta
-normal and obviously not equal to
c
.
This contradicts to the confluence.

–

Otherwise,
J[c/u]I
is
\beta
-normal and not equal to
c
.
This contradicts to the confluence.

Next we show that the planar lambda calculus additionally employing the
\eta
-equality separates
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

{exa}

Suppose three constant symbols
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
and add the following constant rules (
i=1,2,3
) to the construction rules of planar lambda terms.

(constant)

\vdash c_{i}

We assume no additional reduction rules about the constants.
Closed planar terms (that may contain constants) modulo
=_{\beta\eta}
form a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, which we call
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{Pc}
.
Note that the equivalence relation of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{Pc}
is the
\beta\eta
-equality, while that of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Pc}
(Example
4.1
) is the
\beta
-equality.
We have
\lambda xyz.x(yz)
as a representation of
{\bf I}^{\times}
in
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{Pc}
.
Indeed, for any term
M
,
(\lambda xyz.x(yz))M(\lambda w.w)=_{\beta}\lambda z.Mz=_{\eta}M
.

Proposition 32
.

{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{Pc}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra but not a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.
Hence,

{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras
\subsetneq

{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

Proof 4.2
.

Assume that there are some terms
L
and
P
in
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{Pc}
satisfying that for any terms
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
and
M_{3}
,
LM_{1}(PM_{2}M_{3})=_{\beta\eta}M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}
.
Taking
M_{1}=M_{2}=M_{3}:=\lambda x.x
, we see that
L
and
P
cannot contain constants.

Taking
M_{i}:=c_{i}
, we have
Lc_{1}(Pc_{2}c_{3})=_{\beta\eta}c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}
.
Since
L
is a closed planar term with no constants, the
\beta\eta
-normal form of
L
is the form
\lambda xy_{1}\dots y_{m}.xN_{1}\dots N_{n}
(
m,n\geq 0
).
Therefore,
Lc_{1}(Pc_{2}c_{3})=_{\beta\eta}(\lambda y_{1}\dots y_{m}.c_{1}N_{1}\dots N_{n})(Pc_{2}c_{3})
.
However, this term cannot be
\beta\eta
-equal to
c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}
since
c_{1}
cannot receive
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
as separated arguments no matter how the form of
P
is.

Next we show that the freely constructed
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra separates
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras.

{exa}

We take
{\mathcal{F}}
as the freely constructed
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra with two constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.
That is, elements of
{\mathcal{F}}
are constructed from
{\bf B}
,
{\bf I}
,
{\bf I}^{\times}
,
{\bf L}
,
{\bf P}
,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
using the application and the unary operation
{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
.
The equality in
{\mathcal{F}}
is obtained by the axioms of
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and we do not assume any axioms on the constants.

Proposition 33
.

{\mathcal{F}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra but not a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.
Hence,

bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras
\subsetneq

{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

Proof 4.3
.

Assume that
{\mathcal{F}}
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra and write the right and left applications as

\vec{@}

and

\vec{@}

. Here this

\vec{@}

is the same application as that of
{\mathcal{F}}
as a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra, that is,
MN
and
M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N
denote the same element.

By the combinatory completeness, there is an element
M:={\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
in
{\mathcal{F}}
.
Since
M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\bf I}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{{\bf I}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}={\bf I}
holds, this
M
cannot contain
c_{1}
nor
c_{2}
.
For this
M
,
M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}c_{1}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{c_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}=c_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}c_{1}
.
As we can see from the axioms of
{\bf B}
,
{\bf I}
,
{\bf I}^{\times}
,
{\bf L}
,
{\bf P}
and
{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
,
it is impossible for
M
in any form to exchange the order of two arguments
c_{1}
and
{c_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
in
M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}c_{1}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{c_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
.
Then it is also impossible for
{c_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
in any form to reduce
M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}c_{1}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{c_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
to
c_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}c_{1}
.

Finally we show the bi-planar lambda calculus
{\mathcal{L}}_{B}
(Example
3.4
) separates bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras.

Proposition 34
.

{\mathcal{L}}_{B}
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra but not a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.
Hence,

{\bf BCI}
-algebras
\subsetneq
bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras.

Proof 4.4
.

Assume that there is some closed bi-planar lambda term
C
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{B}
such that for any closed bi-planar term
M
,
N
and
L
,
C\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}L=_{\beta}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}L\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N
.
Let
C^{\prime}
be the
\beta
-normal form of
C\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\mapsto$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.

C^{\prime}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N=_{\beta}N\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M
holds for any
M
and
N
.
Take
M:={\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{y}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
and
N:={\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{y}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{z}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\mapsto}\hskip 1.0pt{z}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
.
Note that for any
\beta
-normal term
P
and a free variable
w
of
P
,
P[M/w]
and
P[N/w]
are
\beta
-normal.

•

When
C^{\prime}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M
is
\beta
-normal, both
C^{\prime}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N
and
N\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M
are
\beta
-normal.
However, obviously
C^{\prime}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N\neq_{\beta}N\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M
and it contradicts to the confluence of the bi-planar lambda calculus.

•

When
C^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{C^{\prime\prime}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\mapsto$}}\hskip 1.0pt{u}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
for some
C^{\prime\prime}
and
u
,
C^{\prime}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N=_{\beta}C^{\prime\prime}[M/u]\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N
.

–

When
C^{\prime\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{C^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\mapsto$}}\hskip 1.0pt{v}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
for some
C^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
v
,
C^{\prime}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N=_{\beta}C^{\prime\prime\prime}[M/u][N/v]
.
Since
v
is the rightmost free variable of
C^{\prime\prime\prime}
,
N
is to the right of
M
in
C^{\prime\prime\prime}[M/u][N/v]
.
Hence
C^{\prime\prime\prime}[M/u][N/v]\neq_{\beta}N\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M
and it contradicts to the confluence.

–

Otherwise,
C^{\prime\prime}[M/u]\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N
is
\beta
-normal.

C^{\prime\prime}[M/u]\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N\neq_{\beta}N\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M
and it contradicts to the confluence.

4.2.
The planar lambda calculus is not a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra

Proofs of separations in the previous subsection are straightforward ones.
However, it is sometimes difficult to show that an applicative structure does not belong to certain class of applicative structures.
In this subsection, as an example, we will show that
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
of Example
3.1
(the planar lambda calculus with no constant) is not a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.
Compared to propositions when constants exist (Proposition
31
and
32
), the proof is more tricky.

Lemma 35
.

For any term
M
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
, there is a term
N
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
such that
NM=_{\beta}\lambda x.x
.

Proof 4.5
.

Since planar lambda terms always have
\beta
-normal forms uniquely, we can assume
M
is
\beta
-normal w.l.o.g.
We show this lemma by the induction on the number of bound variables of
M
.
When
BV(M)
is a singleton,
M
is
\lambda x.x
and
N:=\lambda x.x
satisfies
NM=_{\beta}\lambda x.x
.

Assuming that the lemma holds till the number of bound variables of
M
is
k
, we will show that the lemma holds for
M
which contains
k+1
bound variables.
Since
M
is planar and
\beta
-normal,
M=\lambda xy_{1}\dots y_{m}.xP_{1}\dots P_{n}
for
some
\beta
-normal planar terms
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
. Here
y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
are all the free variables of
P_{1},\dots,P_{n}
.
Let
Q_{j}
be the term replacing all the
y_{i}
in
P_{j}
with
\lambda z.z
.
Each
Q_{j}
is a closed planar term and has at most
k
bound variables.
Hence, from the induction hypothesis, there exists some closed planar term
R_{j}
such that
R_{j}Q_{j}=_{\beta}\lambda x.x
.
Take
N^{\prime}:=\lambda w_{1}\dots w_{n}.(R_{1}w_{1})\dots(R_{n}w_{n})
and
N:=\lambda u.uN^{\prime}(\lambda z_{1}.z_{1})\dots(\lambda z_{m}.z_{m})
.
Then
N^{\prime}
and
N
are closed planar terms and

\displaystyle NM

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle MN^{\prime}(\lambda z_{1}.z_{1})\dots(\lambda z_{m}.z_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda xy_{1}\dots y_{m}.xP_{1}\dots P_{n})N^{\prime}(\lambda z_{1}.z_{1})\dots(\lambda z_{m}.z_{m})

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle N^{\prime}Q_{1}\dots Q_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda w_{1}\dots w_{n}.(R_{1}w_{1})\dots(R_{n}w_{n}))Q_{1}\dots Q_{n}

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle(R_{1}Q_{1})\dots(R_{n}Q_{n})

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle(\lambda x.x)\dots(\lambda x.x)

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle\lambda x.x.

Lemma 36
.

{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
is not a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.

Proof 4.6
.

Assume that there is a term
T
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
such that
TMN=_{\beta}NM
for any
M
and
N
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
.
(Note that a total applicative structure containing
{\bf B}
and
{\bf I}
is a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra iff it has
{\bf T}
such that
{\bf T}xy=yx
.
Indeed,
{\bf B}({\bf B}({\bf T}({\bf B}{\bf B}{\bf T})){\bf B}){\bf T}

satisfies the axiom of the
{\bf C}
-combinator.)
Take a term
\lambda xy_{1}\dots y_{m}.xP_{1}\dots P_{n}
as the
\beta
-normal form of
T
.
If
n=0
,
T=\lambda x.x
and it immediately leads contradiction.
Thus
n\geq 1
.

Since
TMN=_{\beta}NM
for any
M
and
N
,

TM=_{\beta}TMT=_{\beta}TMTT=_{\beta}TMTTT=_{\beta}\dots
.

Let
Q_{j}

(j=1,\dots,n)
be the terms replacing all the
y_{i}
in
P_{j}
with
T
.
Each
Q_{j}
is a closed planar term.
Let
U:=\lambda x.xQ_{1}\dots Q_{n}
.

\displaystyle UM

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda x.xQ_{1}\dots Q_{n})M

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle MQ_{1}\dots Q_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(MP_{1}\dots P_{n})[T/y_{1}]\dots[T/y_{m}]

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle(\lambda xy_{1}\dots y_{m}.xP_{1}\dots P_{n})MT\dots T

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle TMT\dots T

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle TM.

Thus
UMN=_{\beta}(TM)N=_{\beta}NM
holds for any
M
and
N
.

From Lemma
35
, there exist closed terms
R_{j}

(j=1,\dots,n)
such that
R_{j}Q_{j}=_{\beta}\lambda z.z
.
Take
M_{0}:=\lambda w_{1}\dots w_{n}.(R_{1}w_{1})\dots(R_{n}w_{n})
.
Then for any closed planar term
N
,

\displaystyle NM_{0}

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle UM_{0}N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda x.xQ_{1}\dots Q_{n})M_{0}N

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle M_{0}Q_{1}\dots Q_{n}N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda w_{1}\dots w_{n}.(R_{1}w_{1})\dots(R_{n}w_{n}))Q_{1}\dots Q_{n}N

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle(R_{1}Q_{1})\dots(R_{n}Q_{n})N

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle(\lambda z.z)\dots(\lambda z.z)N

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle N.

Taking
N_{0}:=\lambda x.x
in
N_{0}M_{0}=_{\beta}N_{0}
, we get
M_{0}=\lambda x.x
.
Therefore,
N(\lambda x.x)=_{\beta}N
holds for any closed planar term
N
.
However,
N:=\lambda y.y(\lambda z.z)
is the counterexample of this equation and it leads contradiction.

Proposition 37
.

{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
is a not a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.

Proof 4.7
.

Assume that
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.
That is, taking

\vec{@}

as the application canonically obtained by the application of planar lambda terms, assume that there is some binary operation

\vec{@}

such that
(|{\mathcal{L}}_{P}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}},\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}})
becomes a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.
This

\vec{@}

is the binary operation not on planar lambda terms, but on
\beta
-equivalence classes of planar lambda terms.
However, in the sequel, we denote a lambda term
M
indistinguishably to the equivalence class containing
M
.
For instance, for planar lambda terms
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
,
M_{1}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}M_{2}
denotes some representation of
\overline{M_{1}}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}\overline{M_{2}}
, where
\overline{M_{i}}
is the
\beta
-equivalence class containing
M_{i}
.

By the combinatory completeness for bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras, there is a closed planar term

L
representing
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
.
Take a term
\lambda xy_{1}\dots y_{m}.xP_{1}\dots P_{n}
as the
\beta
-normal form of
L
.
For a term
T
representing
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$})}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
, dividing to the cases of
n=0
or not, we will show that
T
makes
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra and leads contradiction to Lemma
36
.

If
n=0
,
L=\lambda x.x
and
M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N=_{\beta}(L\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M)\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N=_{\beta}M\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}N
holds for any
M
and
N
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
.
Given arbitrary term
N_{0}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
, take
M:=\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}
and
N:={N_{0}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
in
M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N=_{\beta}M\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}N
.
Then we get

{N_{0}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}=_{\beta}N_{0}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$}
.
For arbitrary
M_{0}
and
N_{0}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
,

\displaystyle T\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M_{0}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$})}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})})\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M_{0}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N_{0}

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle M_{0}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(N_{0}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$})

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle M_{0}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}{N_{0}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle N_{0}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M_{0}

holds.
Hence,
T
makes
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra and contradicts to Lemma
36
.

Next is the case of
n\geq 1
.
Since
L\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N=_{\beta}M\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}N
for any
M
and
N
,

L\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M=_{\beta}L\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}({L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})=_{\beta}L\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}({L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}({L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})=_{\beta}L\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}({L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}({L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}({L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})=_{\beta}\dots.

Let
Q_{j}
be the term replacing all the
y_{i}
in
P_{j}
with
{L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
.
Each
Q_{j}
is a closed planar term.
Let

V:=\lambda x.xQ_{1}\dots Q_{n}
.

\displaystyle VM

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda x.xQ_{1}\dots Q_{n})M

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle MQ_{1}\dots Q_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(MP_{1}\dots P_{n})[{L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}/y_{1}]\dots[{L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}/y_{m}]

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle(\lambda xy_{1}\dots y_{m}.xP_{1}\dots P_{n})M({L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})\dots({L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle LM({L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})\dots({L}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle LM.

Thus
VMN=_{\beta}LMN=_{\beta}M\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}N
holds for any
M
and
N
.

From Lemma
35
, there exists closed term
R_{j}

(j=1,\dots,n)
such that
R_{j}Q_{j}=_{\beta}\lambda z.z
.
Take
M_{1}:=\lambda w_{1}\dots w_{n}.(R_{1}w_{1})\dots(R_{n}w_{n})
.
Then for any closed planar term
N
,

\displaystyle M_{1}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}N

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle LM_{1}N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda xy_{1}\dots y_{m}.xP_{1}\dots P_{n})M_{1}N

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle M_{1}Q_{1}\dots Q_{n}N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda w_{1}\dots w_{n}.(R_{1}w_{1})\dots(R_{n}w_{n}))Q_{1}\dots Q_{n}N

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle(R_{1}Q_{1})\dots(R_{n}Q_{n})N

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle(\lambda z.z)\dots(\lambda z.z)N

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle N.

Taking
N:=\vec{\bf I}
in
M_{1}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}N=_{\beta}N
, we get
M_{1}=\vec{\bf I}
.
Therefore,
\vec{\bf I}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}N_{1}=_{\beta}N_{1}
holds for any closed planar term
N_{1}
.
Given arbitrary
N_{2}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
, with
N_{1}:={N_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
, we get

\displaystyle{N_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle\vec{\bf I}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}{N_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle N_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\vec{\bf I}.

For arbitrary
M_{2}
and
N_{2}
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
,

\displaystyle T\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\vec{\bf I})}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N_{2}

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle M_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(N_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}\vec{\bf I})

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle M_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}{N_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}

\displaystyle=_{\beta}

\displaystyle N_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}M_{2}

holds. Hence,
T
makes
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra and contradicts to Lemma
36
.

We have already seen in Proposition
32
that
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{Pc}
(the planar lambda calculus with constants) is not a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.
However, whether
{\mathcal{L}}_{P}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra is still open.

4.3.
The computational lambda calculus

Next we consider the computational lambda calculus as an applicative structure that gives rise to non-symmetric structures.
The computational lambda calculus is a variant of the lambda calculus whose evaluation rules are sound for programs with computational effects
[
Mog88
]
. The following axiomatization is from
[
Sab96
]
.

{defi}

Suppose infinite supply of variables
x,y,z,\dots
.

Values
,
terms
and
evaluation contexts
are defined as follows:

•

(values)
V::=x\;|\;\lambda x.M

•

(terms)
M::=V\;|\;MM^{\prime}

•

(evaluation contexts)
E[\mathchar 45]::=[\mathchar 45]\;|\;EM\;|\;VE

(Terms are the same ones of the ordinary lambda calculus in Example
2.2
.)

An equivalence relation
=_{c}
on terms is defined as the congruence of the following equations:

•

(
\beta_{V}
)
(\lambda x.M)V=_{c}M[V/x]

•

(
\eta_{V}
)
\lambda x.Vx=_{c}V

•

(
\beta_{\Omega}
)
(\lambda x.E[x])M=_{c}E[M]

Here
E[M]
denotes the term obtained by substituting
M
for
[\mathchar 45]
in
E[\mathchar 45]
.

The
(untyped) computational lambda calculus
is the lambda calculus formed by terms and
=_{c}
.

In
[
Tom21
]
, we showed that the computational lambda calculus is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra but not a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.
We can get a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{C}}
, whose underlying set is equivalence classes of lambda terms modulo
=_{c}
. (Note that terms of
{\mathcal{C}}
are not restricted to closed terms.)
Here
\lambda xyz.x(yz)
,
\lambda x.x
,
\lambda xy.yx
and
\lambda x.xM
are representatives of
{\bf B}
,
{\bf I}
,
{\bf I}^{\times}
and
{M}^{\bullet}
respectively.

Although the computational lambda calculus has all terms of the lambda calculus,
{\mathcal{C}}
is not a PCA nor a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.
This is reasonable considering that programs with effects cannot be discarded, duplicated nor exchanged in general, and thus
{\mathcal{C}}
cannot have the
{\bf S}
/
{\bf K}
/
{\bf C}
-combinator.
Moreover, we can prove the next proposition.

Proposition 38
.

{\mathcal{C}}
is not a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.

To prove this proposition, we use the
CPS-translation

[
SF93
]
.
The CPS-translation
[\hskip-1.5pt[{\mathchar 45}]\hskip-1.5pt]
sends terms of the computational lambda terms to terms of the ordinary lambda calculus and is defined inductively as follows.

•

[\hskip-1.5pt[{x}]\hskip-1.5pt]:=\lambda k.kx

•

[\hskip-1.5pt[{\lambda x.M}]\hskip-1.5pt]:=\lambda k.k(\lambda x.[\hskip-1.5pt[{M}]\hskip-1.5pt])

•

[\hskip-1.5pt[{MN}]\hskip-1.5pt]:=\lambda k.[\hskip-1.5pt[{M}]\hskip-1.5pt](\lambda f.[\hskip-1.5pt[{N}]\hskip-1.5pt](\lambda x.fxk))

For any term
M
and
N
,
M=_{c}N
holds in the computational lambda calculus iff
[\hskip-1.5pt[{M}]\hskip-1.5pt]=_{\beta\eta}[\hskip-1.5pt[{N}]\hskip-1.5pt]
holds in the ordinary lambda calculus.

Proof 4.8

(Proof of Proposition
38
)
.

We will lead a contradiction by assuming
{\mathcal{C}}
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.
If
{\mathcal{C}}
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra, we have a term
L
representing
{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}(}{x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{y}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}}\hskip 1.0pt\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}$}}\hskip 1.0pt{x}{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0})}
and a term
{M}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
representing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}(}{x}\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\overset{\ast}{\mapsto}}\hskip 1.0pt{M\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x}{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698})}
for each term
M
.
For any terms
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
,
LM_{1}({M_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})=_{c}M_{2}M_{1}
holds, and thus
[\hskip-1.5pt[{LM_{1}({M_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})}]\hskip-1.5pt]=_{\beta\eta}[\hskip-1.5pt[{M_{2}M_{1}}]\hskip-1.5pt]
holds.
Now we take a fresh variables
v
and let
M_{2}:=vv
.
Additionally we take a fresh variable (fresh for
L
,
M_{2}
and
{M_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}
)
u
and let
M_{1}:=uu
.
Then

\displaystyle[\hskip-1.5pt[{LM_{1}({M_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})}]\hskip-1.5pt]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda k.(\lambda k^{\prime}.[\hskip-1.5pt[{L}]\hskip-1.5pt](\lambda f^{\prime}.[\hskip-1.5pt[{M_{1}}]\hskip-1.5pt](\lambda x^{\prime}.f^{\prime}x^{\prime}k^{\prime})))(\lambda f.[\hskip-1.5pt[{{M_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}}]\hskip-1.5pt](\lambda x.fxk))

\displaystyle=_{\beta\eta}

\displaystyle\lambda k.[\hskip-1.5pt[{L}]\hskip-1.5pt](\lambda f^{\prime}.[\hskip-1.5pt[{M_{1}}]\hskip-1.5pt](\lambda x^{\prime}.f^{\prime}x^{\prime}(\lambda f.[\hskip-1.5pt[{{M_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}}]\hskip-1.5pt](\lambda x.fxk))))

\displaystyle=_{\beta\eta}

\displaystyle\lambda k.[\hskip-1.5pt[{L}]\hskip-1.5pt](\lambda f^{\prime}.uu(\lambda x^{\prime}.f^{\prime}x^{\prime}(\lambda f.[\hskip-1.5pt[{{M_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}}]\hskip-1.5pt](\lambda x.fxk)))),

\displaystyle[\hskip-1.5pt[{M_{2}M_{1}}]\hskip-1.5pt]

\displaystyle=_{\beta\eta}

\displaystyle\lambda k.vv(\lambda f.uu(\lambda x.fxk)).

In
[\hskip-1.5pt[{M_{2}M_{1}}]\hskip-1.5pt]
,
vv
receives the argument of the form
(\dots uu\dots)
.
However, since
u
and
v
are fresh, no matter what
[\hskip-1.5pt[{L}]\hskip-1.5pt]
is, in
[\hskip-1.5pt[{LM_{1}({M_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})}]\hskip-1.5pt]
,
vv
cannot receive arguments containing
uu
.
Hence these terms
[\hskip-1.5pt[{LM_{1}({M_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}})}]\hskip-1.5pt]
and
[\hskip-1.5pt[{M_{2}M_{1}}]\hskip-1.5pt]
cannot be
\beta\eta
-equal.
It leads a contradiction to the soundness of the CPS-translation.

Semantically, the untyped ordinary/linear/planar lambda calculus is modeled by a reflexive object of a CCC/SMCC/closed multicategory.
And it is related to the categorical structures of assemblies on each lambda calculus.
On the other hand, the untyped computational lambda calculus is modeled by a reflexive object of a Kleisli category.
Since the categorical structure of a Kleisli category is not monoidal in general but premonoidal (See
[
PR97
]
), it is expected that the category of assemblies on the untyped computational lambda calculus is not a monoidal category.
Thus the computational lambda calculus
{\mathcal{C}}
is expected not to be a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra inducing monoidal closed category, however, we have not proven this conjecture yet.

Here, we give an intuitive explanation for the conjecture.
Assume that
{\bf L}
and
{\bf P}
exist in the computational lambda calculus.
Take three non-values
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
and
M_{3}
.
Suppose these terms are reduced to values:
{\bf L}\rightarrow v_{L}
;
{\bf P}\rightarrow v_{P}
;
M_{i}\rightarrow v_{i}
.
In
M_{1}M_{2}M_{3}
, the evaluation proceeds as follows:

M_{1}
is reduced to
v_{1}

\rightsquigarrow

M_{2}
is reduced to
v_{2}

\rightsquigarrow

v_{1}v_{2}
is reduced

\rightsquigarrow

M_{3}
is reduced to
v_{3}

\rightsquigarrow

…

On the other hand, in
{\bf L}M_{1}({\bf P}M_{2}M_{3})
, the evaluation proceeds as follows:

{\bf L}
is reduced to
v_{L}

\rightsquigarrow

M_{1}
is reduced to
v_{1}

\rightsquigarrow

v_{L}v_{1}
is reduced

\rightsquigarrow

{\bf P}
is reduced to
v_{P}

\rightsquigarrow

M_{2}
is reduced to
v_{2}

\rightsquigarrow

v_{P}v_{2}
is reduced

\rightsquigarrow

M_{3}
is reduced to
v_{3}

\rightsquigarrow

…

These two computations seem not to coincide, since the order of the evaluations of
v_{1}v_{2}
and
M_{3}
is reversed.

5.
Necessary conditions for inducing closed structures

We have seen that applicative structures of certain classes induce the corresponding categorical structures, in Proposition
2
(CCCs), Proposition
4
(SMCCs), Proposition
15
(closed multicategories), Proposition
17
(closed categories), Proposition
20
(monoidal closed categories) and Proposition
28
(monoidal bi-closed categories).
In this section, we show the certain “inverses” of these propositions hold.

Proposition 39
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{A}}
is a total applicative structure and
{\mathbb{C}}:=\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
happens to be a CCC.

{\mathcal{A}}
is an
{\bf SK}
-algebra if the following conditions hold.

(1)

|Y^{X}|=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
and
\|{f}\|_{Y^{X}}=\{r\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}
.

(2)

For
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
,
g^{f}:Y^{X}\rightarrow Y^{\prime X^{\prime}}
is the function sending
h:X\rightarrow Y
to
g\circ h\circ f
.

(3)

The forgetful functor from
{\mathbb{C}}
to
{\sf Sets}
strictly preserves finite products.

(4)

The adjunction
\Phi:\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X\times Y,Z)\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Z^{Y})
is the function sending a function
f
to the function
x\mapsto(y\mapsto f(x,y))
.

Proof 5.1
.

Take an object
A:=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\|\mathchar 45\|_{A})
, where
\|{a}\|_{A}:=\{a\}
.
When we take
{\bf I}
as a realizer of
id_{A}
, this
{\bf I}
satisfies
\forall a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf I}\|{a}\|_{A}\subseteq\|{id_{A}(a)}\|_{A}
.
That is,
\forall a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf I}a=a
.

Applying
\Phi
to the first projection
(a,a^{\prime})\mapsto a:A\times A\rightarrow A
,
we get a map
k:A\rightarrow A^{A}
, which sends
a
to
(a^{\prime}\mapsto a)
.
(Here we use the conditions
1
,
4
and
3
to clarify what the function
k
actually is.)
When we take
{\bf K}
as a realizer of
k
, this
{\bf K}
satisfies
\forall a,a^{\prime}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf K}aa^{\prime}=a
.

Let
\phi:A\rightarrow A^{A}
be the function sending
a
to the function
x\mapsto ax
.
Here
\phi(a)
is realized by
a
and
\phi
is realized by
{\bf I}
.

Applying
\Phi
twice to the map from
((A^{A})^{A}\times A^{A})\times A
to
A
defined as

((A^{A})^{A}\times A^{A})\times A\xrightarrow{id\times{\rm diagonal}}((A^{A})^{A}\times A^{A})\times(A\times A)

\xrightarrow{{\rm symmetry}}((A^{A})^{A}\times A)\times(A^{A}\times A)\xrightarrow{ev\times ev}A^{A}\times A\xrightarrow{ev}A,

we get a map
s:(A^{A})^{A}\rightarrow(A^{A})^{(A^{A})}
which sends a function
g:A\rightarrow A^{A}
to the function

(f:A\rightarrow A)\mapsto(a\mapsto g(a)(f(a)))
.
The map

A\xrightarrow{\phi}A^{A}\xrightarrow{{\phi}^{id}}(A^{A})^{A}\xrightarrow{s}(A^{A})^{(A^{A})}\xrightarrow{id^{\phi}}(A^{A})^{A}

is the function

a\mapsto(a^{\prime}\mapsto(a^{\prime\prime}\mapsto aa^{\prime\prime}(a^{\prime}a^{\prime\prime})))
.
(Here we use the conditions
2
to clarify what the functions
{\phi}^{id}
and
{id^{\phi}}
actually are.)
Thus, when we take
{\bf S}
as a realizer of this map,
{\bf S}
satisfies
{\bf S}xyz=xz(yz)
for any
x,y,z\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
.

To rephrase the proposition, to obtain a CCC by categorical realizability, being an
{\bf SK}
-algebra is the necessary condition on the total applicative structure (under several conditions).
We will show the similar propositions for the other classes.
Combining the propositions in this section and the separations in the previous section, we can say that, for instance, the category of assemblies on an applicative structure that is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra but not a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra (
e.g.
,
\sf{Asm}({{\mathcal{L}}_{B}})
) is indeed non-symmetric monoidal (as long as we try to take the symmetry in the canonical way).

Remark 40
.

When we try to prove the proposition replacing “total applicative structure” with “partial applicative structure” in Proposition
39
, we cannot use the same proof.
This is because
\phi:A\rightarrow A^{A}
is not always defined.
Indeed, when
aa^{\prime}
is not defined in
{\mathcal{A}}
,
\phi(a)
is not defined at
a^{\prime}
.
It is still unclear whether we can prove the similar proposition as Proposition
39
when
{\mathcal{A}}
is a partial applicative structure.

.

.

Proposition 42
.

Suppose

-algebra if the following conditions hold.

and

.

Here

\|{f}\|=\{r\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}

(5)

to the function

.

Proposition 43
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{A}}
is a total applicative structure and
{\mathbb{C}}:=\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
happens to be a closed category.

{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra if the following conditions hold.

(1)

|Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X|=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
and
\|{f}\|_{Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X}=\{r\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}
.

(2)

g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f:(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\rightarrow(Y^{\prime}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X^{\prime})
is the function sending
h:X\rightarrow Y
to
g\circ h\circ f
.

(3)

i_{X}
is the function sending a function
(f:\ast\mapsto x)
to
x
.

(4)

L_{Y,Z}^{X}
is the function sending
g:Y\rightarrow Z
to the function
(f:X\rightarrow Y)\mapsto g\circ f
.

Remark 44
.

In the condition
3
, we assume that the unit object is a singleton
\{\ast\}
.
The assumption can be derived from the condition
1
.

Take an object
X:=(\{x_{1},x_{2}\},\|\mathchar 45\|_{X})
by
\|{x_{i}}\|_{X}:=|{\mathcal{A}}|
.
From the condition
1
,

|X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I|
is
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(I,X)
.
Since
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(I,X)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\sf Sets}}(|I|,\{x_{1},x_{2}\})
,
|X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I|=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\sf Sets}}(|I|,\{x_{1},x_{2}\})
.
Also since
X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I\cong X
,
|X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I|\cong|X|=\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
.

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\sf Sets}}(|I|,\{x_{1},x_{2}\})\cong\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
holds iff
|I|
is the singleton.

Proof 5.4

(Proof of Proposition

)
.

, where

, this

.

and

.

(2)

g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f:(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\rightarrow(Y^{\prime}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X^{\prime})
is the function sending
h:X\rightarrow Y
to

.

(3)

{\mathbb{C}}
to

(4)

\Phi:\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X\otimes Y,Z)\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y)

to the function

Proof 5.5
.

Applying
\Phi
twice to the map

((Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\otimes(X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Z))\otimes Z\xrightarrow{{\rm associator}}(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\otimes((X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Z)\otimes Z)\xrightarrow{id\otimes ev}(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\otimes X\xrightarrow{ev}Y,

we get a map
L^{X}_{Y,Z}:(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\rightarrow((Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Z)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Z))
.
This
L
is the natural transformation
L
of the closed category
{\mathbb{C}}
.
Applying
\Phi
to the unitor
\rho_{X}:X\otimes I\rightarrow X
, we get a map
i^{-1}_{X}:X\rightarrow(X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I)
.
The inverse map is the natural isomorphism
i
of the closed category
{\mathbb{C}}
.
We can easily check that
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathbb{C}}
satisfies all the conditions of Proposition
43
for these
L
and
i
.
Hence,
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.

Take an object
A:=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\|\mathchar 45\|_{A})
, where
\|{a}\|_{A}:=\{a\}
.
Let
\phi:A\rightarrow(A\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>A)
be the function sending
a
to the function
x\mapsto ax
.
Here
\phi(a)
is realized by
a
and
\phi
is realized by
{\bf I}
.

Let
l:A\rightarrow(A\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(A\otimes A))
be the map obtained by applying
\Phi
to

A\otimes(A\otimes A)\xrightarrow{{\rm associator}}(A\otimes A)\otimes A\xrightarrow{(\phi\otimes id)\otimes id}((A\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>A)\otimes A)\otimes A

\xrightarrow{ev\otimes id}A\otimes A\xrightarrow{\phi\otimes id}(A\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>A)\otimes A\xrightarrow{ev}A,

and let
{\bf L}
be a realizer of
l
.

l
is the function sending
x
to the function
(y,z)\mapsto xyz
.
Also let
{\bf P}
be a realizer of
p:=\Phi(id_{A\otimes A}):A\rightarrow((A\otimes A)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>A)
.

p
is the function sending
y
to the function
z\mapsto(y,z)
.
Then for any
x,y,z\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,
{\bf L}x({\bf P}yz)\in\|{l(x)(p(y)(z))}\|_{A}
and thus

\displaystyle{\bf L}x({\bf P}yz)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l(x)(p(y)(z))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l(x)(y,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle xyz.

The proof of the next proposition, for monoidal bi-closed categories and bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras, is a little more complicated than the proofs of previous propositions.
When we obtain a monoidal bi-closed category
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
by a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
,
we take realizers of elements of the object
X\multimap Y
in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
as

\|{f}\|_{X\multimap Y}:=\{r\in|{\mathcal{A}}|\mid\mbox{$a\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}r\in\|{f(x)}\|_{Y}$}\}

(See the proof of Proposition
28
).
However, in the next proposition we do not assume anything about the left application of
{\mathcal{A}}
, and thus we also cannot assume anything about realizers for
X\multimap Y
.
This makes the proof of existence for
\vec{\bf B}
and
\vec{\bf D}
cumbersome.

.

Proof 5.6
.

The conditions of this proposition includes all the conditions of Proposition
45
.
Hence,
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra and have the combinatory completeness for the planar lambda calculus.
We take

\vec{\reflectbox{$\bf B$}}

-combinator and

\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}

as the

-combinator of
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Take an object
A:=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\|\mathchar 45\|_{A})
, where
\|{a}\|_{A}:=\{a\}
.
Applying
\Phi
to the evaluation map

ev_{1}:A\otimes(A\multimap A)\rightarrow A
, we get a map
l:A\rightarrow(A\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(A\multimap A))
, which sends
a
to
(f\mapsto f(a))
.
Let
{\bf L}_{1}
be a realizer of
l
and
x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}y:={\bf L}_{1}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y
.
We will show that
(|{\mathcal{A}}|,\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}},\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}})
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra.

Let

be the function sending

to the function

Here

Given arbitrary

.
For any

,

Given arbitrary

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x.

Furthermore, we can take

{(\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{\bf I}}$})}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}\triangleright}}

, whose realizer is

x\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
,

\displaystyle x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}\vec{\bf D})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}({\bf L}_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf L}_{1}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}x\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}({\bf L}_{2}\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}N_{2})

,

\displaystyle=
