\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x\scalebox{0.95}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.447,0.698}$\vec{@}$}}(y\scalebox{0.95}{\reflectbox{{\color[rgb]{0.835,0.368,0}$\vec{@}$}}}z).

In this section we showed propositions for the necessary conditions to obtain certain structures on categories of assemblies.
Next, consider whether the similar propositions hold for the cases of categories of modest sets.
The next propositions can be proven in the same way as Proposition
39
,
42
and
43
.

Proposition 47
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{A}}
is a total applicative structure and
{\mathbb{C}}:=\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
happens to be a CCC.

{\mathcal{A}}
is an
{\bf SK}
-algebra if the following conditions hold.

(1)

|Y^{X}|=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
and
\|{f}\|_{Y^{X}}=\{r\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}
.

(2)

For
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
,
g^{f}:Y^{X}\rightarrow Y^{\prime X^{\prime}}
is the function sending
h:X\rightarrow Y
to
g\circ h\circ f
.

(3)

The forgetful functor from
{\mathbb{C}}
to
{\sf Sets}
strictly preserves finite products.

(4)

The adjunction
\Phi:\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X\times Y,Z)\rightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Z^{Y})
is the function sending a function
f
to the function
x\mapsto(y\mapsto f(x,y))
. ∎

Proposition 48
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{A}}
is a total applicative structure and
{\mathbb{C}}:=\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
happens to be a closed multicategory.

{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra if the following conditions hold.

(1)

{\mathbb{C}}(;X)=|X|
and
\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(;X)=X
.

(2)

{\mathbb{C}}(X;Y)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
and
\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X;Y)=(\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y),\|\mathchar 45\|)
, where

\|{f}\|=\{r\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}
.

(3)

\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)=\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{1};\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{2},\dots,X_{n};Y))
and
{\mathbb{C}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)
is the underlying set of
\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y)
.

(4)

For
g:Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\rightarrow Z
and
f_{l}:X^{l}_{1},\dots,X^{l}_{k_{l}}\rightarrow Y_{l}
,
g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
is the function sending
x^{1}_{1},\dots,x^{1}_{k_{1}},\dots,x^{n}_{k_{n}}
to
g(f_{1}(x^{1}_{1},\dots,x^{1}_{k_{1}}),\dots,f_{n}(x^{n}_{1},\dots,x^{n}_{k_{n}}))
.
When
k_{l}=0
for some
1\leq l\leq n
,
g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
is the function given
y_{l}\in|Y_{l}|
pointed by
f_{l}
as the
l
-th argument of
g
.

(5)

ev_{X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y}
sends
f,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
to
f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.

(6)

\Lambda_{Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m};X_{1},\dots,X_{n};Y}
sends a function
(z_{1},\dots,z_{m},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\mapsto f(z_{1},\dots,z_{m},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))
to the function
(z_{1},\dots,z_{m}\mapsto f(z_{1},\dots,z_{m},-,\dots,-))
. ∎

Proposition 49
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{A}}
is a total applicative structure and
{\mathbb{C}}:=\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
happens to be a closed category.

{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra if the following conditions hold.

(1)

|Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X|=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
and
\|{f}\|_{Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X}=\{r\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}
.

(2)

g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f:(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\rightarrow(Y^{\prime}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X^{\prime})
is the function sending
h:X\rightarrow Y
to
g\circ h\circ f
.

(3)

The underlying set of the unit object
I
is the singleton
\{\ast\}
.

(4)

i_{X}
is the function sending a function
(f:\ast\mapsto x)
to
x
.

(5)

L_{Y,Z}^{X}
is the function sending
g:Y\rightarrow Z
to the function
(f:X\rightarrow Y)\mapsto g\circ f
.

∎

Here note that Proposition
49
has one more condition, that the underlying set of the unit object is a singleton, than Proposition
43
.
This is because the assembly
X
we used in Remark
44
is not a modest set.

On the other hand, for the cases of SMCCs, monoidal closed categories and monoidal bi-closed categories, we cannot state propositions for modest sets similar to Proposition
41
,
45
and
46
.
Since we define tensor products in categories of modest sets in the different way from those of categories of assemblies (as seen in the proof of Proposition
5
), the condition “the forgetful functor from
{\mathbb{C}}
to
{\sf Sets}
is strict monoidal” is not appropriate for the case of modest sets.

For the case of SMCCs, we can avoid this problem by presenting a more generalized proposition, that is for symmetric closed categories, instead of SMCCs.
A
symmetric closed category
is a closed category with a natural isomorphism

S_{X,Y,Z}:(Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X\cong(Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y

satisfying appropriate axioms (
cf.

[
DL78
]
).

Proposition 50
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{A}}
is a total applicative structure and
{\mathbb{C}}:=\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(or
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) happens to be a symmetric closed category.

{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra if the following conditions hold.

(1)

|Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X|=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{C}}}(X,Y)
and
\|{f}\|_{Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X}=\{r\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}
.

(2)

g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f:(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\rightarrow(Y^{\prime}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X^{\prime})
is the function sending
h:X\rightarrow Y
to
g\circ h\circ f
.

(3)

L_{Y,Z}^{X}
is the function sending
g:Y\rightarrow Z
to the function
(f:X\rightarrow Y)\mapsto g\circ f
.

(4)

S_{X,Y,Z}
is the function sending
f:x\mapsto(y\mapsto f(x)(y))
to
S(f):y\mapsto(x\mapsto f(y)(x))
.

This proposition also shows that we cannot obtain
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(or
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) that is a symmetric closed category but not an SMCC, in the canonical way.

For the cases of monoidal closed categories and monoidal bi-closed categories, it is still not clear that there are any appropriate conditions to state propositions for modest sets similar to Proposition
45
and
46
.

6.
Planar linear combinatory algebras

In Section
2.5
, we recalled LCAs and rLCAs, that relate
{\bf BCI}
-algebras and PCAs, and that induce categorical models of linear exponential modalities.
In this section, we apply the similar construction to
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.
We reformulate rLCAs for
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and PCAs, and call them
exp-rPLCAs
.
From an exp-rPLCA, we get a categorical model of
!
-modality on the non-symmetric multiplicative intuitionistic linear logic (MILL).
Also we reformulate rLCAs for
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras, and call them
exch-rPLCAs
.
From an exch-rPLCA, we obtain a model for an exchange modality relating the non-symmetric MILL and the symmetric MILL.

In
[
Tom22
]
, we already introduced the same construction called “rPLCAs,” based on bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras.
What defined as rPLCAs in this section are generalizations of those in
[
Tom22
]
, based on
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

6.1.
Exponential planar linear combinatory algebras

Linear exponential comonads on non-symmetric monoidal categories are investigated in
[
Has16
]
, which model
!
-modalities on non-symmetric MILL.

{defi}

A
linear exponential comonad
on a monoidal category
{\mathbb{C}}
consists of the following data.

•

A monoidal comonad
(!,\delta,\epsilon,m,m_{I})
. Here
!
is an endofunctor on
{\mathbb{C}}
,

\delta_{X}:!X\rightarrow!!X
and
\epsilon:!X\rightarrow X
are monidal natural transformations for the comultiplication and the counit. A natural transformation
m_{X,Y}:!X\otimes!Y\rightarrow!(X\otimes Y)
and a map
m_{I}:I\rightarrow!I
make
!
be a monoidal functor.

•

Monoidal natural transformations
e_{X}:!X\rightarrow I
and
d_{X}:!X\rightarrow!X\otimes!X
.

•

A monidal natural transformation
\sigma_{X,Y}:!X\otimes!Y\rightarrow!Y\otimes!X
defined as

!X\otimes!Y\xrightarrow{\delta_{X}\otimes\delta_{Y}}!!X\otimes!!Y\xrightarrow{m_{!X,!Y}}!(!X\otimes!Y)\xrightarrow{d_{!X\otimes!Y}}!(!X\otimes!Y)\otimes!(!X\otimes!Y)

\xrightarrow{!(e_{X}\otimes id)\otimes!(id\otimes e_{Y})}!(I\otimes!Y)\otimes!(!X\otimes I)\xrightarrow{!({\rm unitor})\otimes!({\rm unitor})}!!Y\otimes!!X\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{!Y}\otimes\epsilon_{!X}}!Y\otimes!X.

Here these components need satisfy the following conditions.

(1)

The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{!X\otimes!X\otimes!Y\otimes!Y\otimes!Z\otimes!Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\otimes\sigma\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{id\otimes\sigma\otimes id}
\textstyle{!X\otimes!Y\otimes!X\otimes!Y\otimes!Z\otimes!Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m\otimes m\otimes id}
\textstyle{!X\otimes!X\otimes!Y\otimes!Z\otimes!Y\otimes!Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\otimes m\otimes m}
\textstyle{!(X\otimes Y)\otimes!(X\otimes Y)\otimes!Z\otimes!Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\otimes\sigma\otimes id}
\textstyle{!X\otimes!X\otimes!(Y\otimes Z)\otimes!(Y\otimes Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\otimes\sigma\otimes id}
\textstyle{!(X\otimes Y)\otimes!Z\otimes!(X\otimes Y)\otimes!Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m\otimes m}
\textstyle{!X\otimes!(Y\otimes Z)\otimes!X\otimes!(Y\otimes Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m\otimes m}
\textstyle{!(X\otimes Y\otimes Z)\otimes!(X\otimes Y\otimes Z)}

(2)

m_{!Y,!X}\circ\sigma_{!X,!Y}=!\sigma_{X,Y}\circ m_{!X,!Y}
.

(3)

\sigma_{X,Y}^{-1}=\sigma_{Y,X}
.

(4)

The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{!X\otimes!Y\otimes!Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{X}\otimes\delta_{Y}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{id\otimes\sigma_{Y,Z}}
\textstyle{!!X\otimes!!Y\otimes!Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{!X,!Y}\otimes id}
\textstyle{!(!X\otimes!Y)\otimes!Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{!X\otimes!Y,Z}}
\textstyle{!X\otimes!Z\otimes!Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{X,Z}\otimes id}
\textstyle{!Z\otimes!(!X\otimes!Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\otimes\epsilon_{!X\otimes!Y}}
\textstyle{!Z\otimes!X\otimes!Y}

(5)

The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{!X\otimes!Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{X}\otimes d_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{m_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{!X\otimes!X\otimes!Y\otimes!Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\otimes\sigma\otimes id}
\textstyle{!X\otimes!Y\otimes!X\otimes!Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m\otimes m}
\textstyle{!(X\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{X\otimes Y}}
\textstyle{!(X\otimes Y)\otimes!(X\otimes Y)}

(6)

The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{I}\otimes m_{I}}
\scriptstyle{m_{I}}
\textstyle{!I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{I}}
\textstyle{!I\otimes!I}

(7)

(!X,e_{X},d_{X})
is a comonoid in
{\mathbb{C}}
.

(8)

e_{X}
and
d_{X}
are coalgebra morphisms.

(9)

\delta_{X}
is a comonoid morphism.

Then we will introduce the categorical realizability to inducing linear exponential comonads on non-symmetric monoidal categories.
The results are reformulations of a part of contents in
[
Hos07
]
and
[
Tom22
]
to the case of
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.

{defi}

An
exponential relational planar linear combinatory algebra (exp-rPLCA)
consists of a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
and a comonadic applicative morphism
(\mathbbold{\exm},{\bf e},{\bf d})
on
{\mathcal{A}}
which satisfies the followings.

•

There is
{\bf k}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
{\bf k}x(\mathbbold{\exm}y)\subseteq\{x\}
for any
x,y\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
.

•

There is
{\bf w}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
{\bf w}x(\mathbbold{\exm}y)\subseteq x(\mathbbold{\exm}y)(\mathbbold{\exm}y)
for any
x,y\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
.

While the above definition employs the different style from rLCAs of Definition
2.5
, we can also define exp-rPLCAs in the same style.

Proposition 51
.

For a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
and a comonadic applicative morphism
(\mathbbold{\exm},{\bf e},{\bf d})
on
{\mathcal{A}}
, the followings are equivalent.

(1)

({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
is an exp-rPLCA.

(2)

Take two total relations
[\mathbbold{\exm},\mathbbold{\exm}]:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
and
k_{i}:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
as
[\mathbbold{\exm},\mathbbold{\exm}](x):=\{{\bf P}aa^{\prime}\mid a,a^{\prime}\in\mathbbold{\exm}x\}
and
k_{i}(x):=\{{\bf I}\}
.
Then they are applicative morphisms and
\mathbbold{\exm}\preceq[\mathbbold{\exm},\mathbbold{\exm}]
and
\mathbbold{\exm}\preceq k_{i}
hold.

Proof 6.1
.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2):
Realizers of
[\mathbbold{\exm},\mathbbold{\exm}]
and
k_{i}
exist as
{\lambda}^{\ast}pq.{\bf w}{\bf P}(r_{\mathbbold{\exm}}({\bf L}{\bf k}p)({\bf L}{\bf k}q))
and
{\bf I}
.
Realizers for
\mathbbold{\exm}\preceq[\mathbbold{\exm},\mathbbold{\exm}]
and
\mathbbold{\exm}\preceq k_{i}
are
{\bf w}{\bf P}
and
{\bf k}{\bf I}
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1):
Take a realizer
r_{1}
of
\mathbbold{\exm}\preceq[\mathbbold{\exm},\mathbbold{\exm}]
and a realizer
r_{2}
of
\mathbbold{\exm}\preceq k_{i}
.
Then
{\bf k}
and
{\bf w}
exist as
{\lambda}^{\ast}xy.{\bf I}^{\times}x(r_{2}y)
and
{\lambda}^{\ast}xy.{\bf L}x(r_{1}y)
.

From an exp-rPLCA, we get a linear exponential comonad.

Proposition 52
.

For an exp-rPLCA
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
,
{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}
is a linear exponential comonad on
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.

Proof 6.2
.

•

It is easy to see that the comultiplication
\delta
and the counit
\epsilon
are monoidal natural transformations.
From Proposition
22
, the comonad
{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}
is a lax monoidal functor and thus we have
m_{X,Y}:{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}X\otimes{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}Y\rightarrow{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}(X\otimes Y)
and
m_{I}:I\rightarrow{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}I
.
Therefore, we have
{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}
as a monoidal comonad.

•

e_{X}:{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}X\rightarrow I
is the function sending
x
to
\ast
. A realizer for
e_{X}
is
{\bf k}{\bf I}
.

•

d_{X}:{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}X\rightarrow{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}X\otimes{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}X
is the function sending
x
to
x\otimes x
.
A realizer for
d_{X}
is
{\bf w}({\lambda}^{\ast}pq.{\bf P}pq)
.

•

It is easy to see that the
({\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast},e_{X},d_{X})
satisfies conditions for linear exponential comonads.

Next we try to obtain linear-non-linear models for the non-symmetric MILL, that is, monoidal adjunctions between (non-symmetric) monoidal closed categories and CCCs.
Although now we get a linear exponential comonad
{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}
on
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
, at this point it has not concluded that we obtain a linear-non-linear model, since we have not shown that the co-Kleisli adjunction between
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}}
is a monoidal adjunction.
To show this, we use the next proposition shown in
[
Has16
]
.

Proposition 53
.

Let
{\mathbb{C}}
be a monoidal closed category and
!
be a linear exponential comonad on
{\mathbb{C}}
. When
{\mathbb{C}}
has finite products, the co-Kleisli category
{\mathbb{C}}_{!}
is a CCC and the co-Kleisli adjunction is monoidal.

Proposition 54
.

For an exp-rPLCA
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
has Cartesian products, and thus the co-Kleisli adjunction between
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and a CCC
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}}
is monoidal.

Proof 6.3
.

•

The terminal object is
(\{\ast\},\|\mathchar 45\|)
, where
\|{\ast}\|:=|{\mathcal{A}}|
.

•

The underlying set of
X\times Y
is
|X|\times|Y|
. Realizers are defined as

\|{(x,y)}\|:=\{\ {\bf P}({\bf P}uv)a\ \mid\ \mbox{$\exists p,\exists q$, $u\in{\mathbbold{\exm}}p$, $v\in{\mathbbold{\exm}}q$, $pa\in\|{x}\|_{X}$ and $qa\in\|{y}\|_{Y}$}\ \}.

The set of realizers is not empty since for
m\in\|{x}\|_{X}
and
m^{\prime}\in\|{y}\|_{Y}
,

{\bf P}({\bf P}(\mathbbold{\exm}({\bf k}m))(\mathbbold{\exm}({\bf k}m^{\prime})))(\mathbbold{\exm}{\bf I})\in\|{(x,y)}\|.

•

For maps
f:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
,
f\times g
is the function sending
(x,y)
to
(f(x),g(y))
.
A realizer of
f\times g
is

{\lambda}^{\ast}uv.{\bf P}({\bf P}(r_{\mathbbold{\exm}}Mu)(r_{\mathbbold{\exm}}Nv))
, where
M\in\mathbbold{\exm}({\bf B}r_{f})
and

N\in\mathbbold{\exm}({\bf B}r_{g})
.

•

A realizer for the projection
\pi:X\times Y\rightarrow X
is

{\bf L}({\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}uv.{\bf e}({\bf k}uv)))
.
A realizer for the projection
\pi^{\prime}:X\times Y\rightarrow Y
is

{\bf L}({\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}uv.{\bf e}({\bf k}{\bf I}uv)))
.

•

For any object
Z
and any maps
f:Z\rightarrow X
and
g:Z\rightarrow Y
, there exists a unique map

h:Z\rightarrow X\times Y
such that
\pi\circ h=f
and
\pi^{\prime}\circ h=g
.

h
is the function sending
z
to
(f(z),g(z))
, whose realizer is in

{\bf P}({\bf P}(\mathbbold{\exm}r_{f})(\mathbbold{\exm}r_{g}))
.

For an exp-rPLCA
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
, we can restrict
{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}:\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
to the comonad on
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
, as we saw in Remark
8
.
By the same proof as the above, we also can get a linear-non-linear model using
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
.

Proposition 55
.

For an exp-rPLCA
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
,
{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}
is a linear exponential comonad on
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
.
Moreover, the co-Kleisli adjunction between the monoidal closed category
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
and the CCC
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\mathbbold{\exm}}_{\ast}}
is monoidal. ∎

We have seen the co-Kleisli adjunctions obtained by an exp-rPLCA
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
are linear-non-linear models by showing that
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
have Cartesian products.
We can further show that these categories have better structures as the next proposition says.

Proposition 56
.

For an exp-rPLCA
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
are finitely complete and finitely cocomplete.

Proof 6.4
.

First we show the proposition for
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
.

•

The terminal object and binary products are those in the proof of Proposition
54
.

•

Given maps
f,g:X\rightarrow Y
, let
Z
be an assembly defined as
|Z|:=\{x\in|X|\mid f(x)=g(x)\}
and
\|{x}\|_{Z}:=\|{x}\|_{X}
.
Take a map
e:Z\rightarrow X
as the inclusion function, realized by
{\bf I}
.
Then it is easy to see that this
e
is the equalizer of
f
and
g
.

•

The initial object is the empty set.

•

Given maps
f,g:X\rightarrow Y
, take a set
|W|:=Y/\sim
, where
\sim
is the smallest equivalence relation satisfying
\forall x\in|X|,f(x)\sim g(x)
.
Take an assembly
W=(|W|,\|\mathchar 45\|_{W})
by
\|{w}\|_{W}:=\bigcup_{y\in w}{\|{y}\|_{Y}}
.
Take a map
e^{\prime}:Y\rightarrow W
by the projection, realized by
{\bf I}
.
Then it is easy to see that this
e^{\prime}
is the coequalizer of
f
and
g
.

•

The underlying set of
X+Y
is
\{(0,x)\mid x\in|X|\}\cup\{(1,y)\mid y\in|Y|\}
.
Realizers are defined as

\|{(0,x)}\|:=\{{\bf P}mp\mid p\in\|{x}\|_{X}\}

and

\|{(1,y)}\|:=\{{\bf P}nq\mid q\in\|{y}\|_{Y}\}
,

where

m:={\lambda}^{\ast}uv.{\bf I}^{\times}({\bf e}u)({\bf k}{\bf I}v)
and
n:={\lambda}^{\ast}uv.{\bf k}{\bf I}u({\bf e}v)
.

The coprojections
in_{X}:X\rightarrow X+Y
and

in_{Y}:Y\rightarrow X+Y
are given as
x\mapsto(0,x)
and
y\mapsto(1,y)
, and realized by
{\bf P}m
and
{\bf P}n
respectively.
Given maps
f:X\rightarrow Z
and
g:Y\rightarrow Z
realized by
r_{f}
and
r_{g}
, we have a unique map
h:X+Y\rightarrow Z
such that
h\circ in_{X}=f
and
h\circ in_{Y}=g
.

h
is the function sending
(0,x)
to
f(x)
and
(1,y)
to
g(y)
, which is realized by
{\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}uv.u(\mathbbold{\exm}r_{f})(\mathbbold{\exm}r_{g})v)
.

Therefore,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is finitely complete and finitely cocomplete.

Since
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
is the reflexive full subcategory of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
,
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
is also finitely complete and finitely cocomplete.

As an adjoint pair between a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra and a PCA gives rise to an rLCA, an adjoint pair between a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra and a PCA gives rise to an exp-rPLCA and a monidal adjunction.

Proposition 57
.

Let
(\delta\dashv\gamma):{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
be an adjoint pair for a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
and a PCA
{\mathcal{B}}
.

(1)

({\mathcal{A}},\delta\circ\gamma)
forms an exp-rPLCA.

(2)

({\delta}_{\ast}\dashv{\gamma}_{\ast}):\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{B}})
is a monoidal adjunction between the monoidal category
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and the Cartesian monoidal category
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{B}})
.

Proof 6.5
.

(1)

From Proposition
7
(
2
),
\delta\circ\gamma
is a comonadic applicative morphism.
Let
{\bf e}
and
{\bf d}
be elements for the counit and the comultiplication.
Then we can take
{\bf k}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
as an element of
{\lambda}^{\ast}xy.{\bf L}x({\bf e}(r_{\delta}(\delta M)y))
, where
M\in{\lambda}^{\ast}z.(\gamma{\bf I})
.
Also we can take
{\bf w}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
as an element of
{\lambda}^{\ast}xy.{\bf L}x({\bf e}(r_{\delta}(\delta N)({\bf d}y)))
, where
N\in{\lambda}^{\ast}z.r_{\gamma}(r_{\gamma}(\gamma{\bf P})z)z
.

(2)

We show that the left adjoint
{\delta}_{\ast}
is strong monoidal.
Let
{\bf e}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
and
{\bf i}\in|{\mathcal{B}}|
be elements such that
\forall a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|,{\bf e}(\delta(\gamma a))=a
and
\forall b\in|{\mathcal{B}}|,{\bf i}b\in\gamma(\delta b)
.
The map
I\rightarrow{\delta}_{\ast}1
is realized by
{(\delta{\bf K})}^{\bullet}
.
The inverse
{\delta}_{\ast}1\rightarrow I
is realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}a.{\bf e}(r_{\delta}(\delta({\lambda}^{\ast}b.(\gamma{\bf I})))a)
.
The natural transformation
({\delta}_{\ast}X)\otimes({\delta}_{\ast}Y)\rightarrow{\delta}_{\ast}(X\times Y)
is realized by

{\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}aa^{\prime}.r_{\delta}(r_{\delta}(\delta({\lambda}^{\ast}bb^{\prime}t.tbb^{\prime}))a)a^{\prime})
.
The inverse map
{\delta}_{\ast}(X\times Y)\rightarrow({\delta}_{\ast}X)\otimes({\delta}_{\ast}Y)
is realized by

{\lambda}^{\ast}u.{\bf e}(r_{\delta}(\delta M)u)
, where

M\in{\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}bb^{\prime}.r_{\gamma}(r_{\gamma}(\gamma{\bf P})({\bf i}b))({\bf i}b^{\prime}))
.

Next we consider the functional case of exp-rPLCAs, like LCAs are the functional case of rLCAs.

{defi}

An
exponential planar linear combinatory algebra
(exp-PLCA) is an exp-rPLCA
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
that
\mathbbold{\exm}
is functional.

Not only are exp-PLCAs special cases of exp-rPLCAs, but also can induce adjoint pairs between
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and PCAs.

Proposition 58
.

Let
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
be an exp-PLCA.

(1)

We have a PCA
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbbold{\exm}}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,@)
with
x@y:=x(\mathbbold{\exm}y)
.

(2)

Let
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbbold{\exm}}
be the identity function and
\delta:{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbbold{\exm}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
be the function
x\mapsto\mathbbold{\exm}x
. Then
\gamma
and
\delta
are applicative morphisms and
\delta\dashv\gamma
.

Proof 6.6
.

(1)

We have the
{\bf K}
-combinator in
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbbold{\exm}}
as
{\lambda}^{\ast}xy.{\bf e}({\bf k}xy)
.
We have the
{\bf S}
-combinator as
{\lambda}^{\ast}xyz.{\bf w}(Mx)(r_{\mathbbold{\exm}}(r_{\mathbbold{\exm}}(\mathbbold{\exm}{\bf P})({\bf d}y))({\bf d}z))
, where

M:={\lambda}^{\ast}xyz.{\bf e}x({\bf L}({\bf k}{\bf I})({\bf e}y))({\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}uv.r_{\mathbbold{\exm}}u({\bf d}v))({\bf e}z))
.

(2)

Realizers of
\gamma
and
\delta
are
{\lambda}^{\ast}xy.x({\bf e}y)
and
{\lambda}^{\ast}xy.r_{\mathbbold{\exm}}x({\bf d}y)
. A realizer for
\delta\circ\gamma\preceq id_{{\mathcal{A}}}
is
{\bf e}
and for
id_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbbold{\exm}}}\preceq\gamma\circ\delta
is
{\bf I}
.

Next we give an (functional) adjoint pair between a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra and a PCA.
This example is a reformulation of the linear lambda calculus with
!
(
cf.

[
Sim05
]
) to a planar variant.

{exa}

Suppose infinite supply of variables
x,y,z,\dots
.
Terms are defined grammatically as follows.

M::=x\;|\;MM^{\prime}\;|\;\lambda x.M\;|\;M\otimes M^{\prime}\;|\;\mbox{{\sf let} ${x\otimes x^{\prime}}$ {\sf be} ${M}$ {\sf in} ${M^{\prime}}$}\;|\;!M\;|\;\lambda!x.M

Here
x
of
\lambda x.M
is the rightmost free variable of
M
, appears exactly once in
M
and is not in any scope of
!
.
Also we assume that for
let

{x\otimes x^{\prime}}

be

{M}

in

{M^{\prime}}
,
x^{\prime}
and
x
are the rightmost and the next rightmost free variables of
N
, appear exactly once in
N
and are not in any scope of
!
.
Take an equational relation on terms as the congruence of the following equational axioms.

•

(\lambda x.M)N=M[N/x]
.

•

M=\lambda x.Mx
.

•

(\lambda!x.M)(!N)=M[N/x]
.

•

\mbox{{\sf let} ${x\otimes x^{\prime}}$ {\sf be} ${M\otimes M^{\prime}}$ {\sf in} ${N}$}=N[M/x][M^{\prime}/x^{\prime}]
.

•

M=\mbox{{\sf let} ${x\otimes y}$ {\sf be} ${M}$ {\sf in} ${x\otimes y}$}
.

Let
\Lambda
be the set of equivalence classes of closed terms.

Then we get a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, whose underlying set is
\Lambda
and the application is that of lambda terms.
Also we get a PCA
{\mathcal{B}}=(\Lambda,@)
, where
M@N:=M(!N)
.
Here the
{\bf K}
-combinator and the
{\bf S}
-combinator of
{\mathcal{B}}
exist as
\lambda!x.\lambda!y.x
and
\lambda!x.\lambda!y.\lambda!z.x(!z)(!(y(!z)))
.

Take an applicative morphism
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
as the identity function whose realizer is
\lambda!x.\lambda!y.xy
.
Take
\delta:{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
as a function
M\mapsto!M
whose realizer is
\lambda!x.\lambda!y.!(x(!y))
. Then we have an adjoint pair
\delta\dashv\gamma
.

As well as we can construct an LCA from a “reflexive object” in a “weak linear category” (See
[
AHS02
]
and
[
Hag00
]
), we can get exp-PLCAs by appropriate settings.

{defi}

A
weak planar linear category (WPLC)
consists of:

(1)

a monoidal closed category
({\mathbb{C}},\otimes,I)
(not symmetric in general);

(2)

a monoidal functor
(!,m,m_{I})
on
{\mathbb{C}}
;

(3)

a monoidal pointwise natural transformation
!\rightarrow id_{{\mathbb{C}}}
;

(4)

a monoidal pointwise natural transformation
!\rightarrow!!
;

(5)

a monoidal pointwise natural transformation
!\rightarrow!\otimes!
;

(6)

a monoidal pointwise natural transformation
!\rightarrow K_{I}
, where
K_{I}
is the constant
I
functor.

Here a
pointwise natural transformation

\gamma:F\rightarrow G
is a family of maps
\gamma_{C}:F(C)\rightarrow G(C)

(
C\in Ob({\mathbb{C}})
) satisfying that
G(f)\circ\gamma_{I}=\gamma_{C}\circ F(f)
for any
f:I\rightarrow C
.

To be a WPLC, we need not all of the conditions for linear exponential comonads (Definition
6.1
).
For instance, a WPLC does not require that
!
is a comonad, and does not require the (ordinary) naturality of each transformation.

{defi}

Let
({\mathbb{C}},!)
be a WPLC.
We say
V
is a
reflexive object
when there are:

(1)

a retraction
p:!V\triangleleft V:q
;

(2)

an isomorphism
r:(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\rightarrow V
and
s:=r^{-1}
;

(3)

a retraction
t:(V\otimes V)\triangleleft V:u
.

As we saw in Example
3.3
, for a reflexive object
V
of a WPLC
{\mathbb{C}}
,
|{\mathcal{A}}|:={\mathbb{C}}(I,V)
forms a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Furthermore, by giving
\mathbbold{\exm}
as an endofunction sending
M:I\rightarrow V
to

p\circ(!M)\circ m_{I}
,
({\mathcal{A}},\mathbbold{\exm})
becomes an exp-PLCA.
The proof is the same as for WLCs and LCAs in
[
Hag00
]
.

6.2.
Exchange planar linear combinatory algebras

Exchange modalities on the Lambek calculus and their categorical models are introduced in
[
JIdP18
]
.
While the word “Lambek calculus” may indicate various logics, type systems or grammars (
cf.

[
Lam58
,
MR12
]
), here we call the Lambek calculus as a variant of non-symmetric MILL with left and right implications.
The Lambek calculus is modeled by monoidal bi-closed categories.
While the order of arguments cannot be exchanged in the Lambek calculus, the Lambek calculus can be extended to a sequent calculus that allows swapping arguments with modalities.
This sequent calculus is called the
commutative/non-commutative (CNC) logic
, that is composed of two (commutative and non-commutative) logics, and the
exchange modality
connects these two parts.
Categorical models of the CNC logic are given as monoidal adjunctions between monoidal bi-closed categories and SMCCs, that are called
Lambek adjoint models
.
In this subsection, we introduce the similar construction to the previous subsection, inducing Lambek adjoint models.

{defi}

An
exchange relational planar linear combinatory algebra
(exch-rPLCA) consists of a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
and a comonadic applicative morphism
(\xi,{\bf e},{\bf d})
on
{\mathcal{A}}
with
{\bf c}\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
satisfying
{\bf c}x(\xi y)(\xi z)\subseteq x(\xi z)(\xi y)
for any
x,y,z\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
.
When
\xi
is functional, we call
({\mathcal{A}},\xi)
an
exchange planar linear combinatory algebra
(exch-PLCA).

Proposition 59
.

For an exch-rPLCA
({\mathcal{A}},\xi)
, the co-Kleisli category
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
is an SMCC and the co-Kleisli adjunction between
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
is monoidal.

Proof 6.7
.

•

We define tensor products in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
as
X\widehat{\otimes}Y:=(|X|\times|Y|,\|\mathchar 45\|)
, where

\|{x\widehat{\otimes}y}\|:=\{{\bf P}pq\mid\mbox{$p\in\xi\|{x}\|_{X}$ and $q\in\xi\|{y}\|_{Y}$}\}.

•

For maps
f:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
,
f\widehat{\otimes}g
is the function sending
x\widehat{\otimes}y
to
f(x)\widehat{\otimes}g(y)
.
A realizer of
f\widehat{\otimes}g
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}z.{\bf L}M({\bf e}z)
, where

M\in{\lambda}^{\ast}pq.{\bf P}(r_{\xi}(\xi r_{f})({\bf d}p))(r_{\xi}(\xi r_{g})({\bf d}q))
.

•

We define the unit object
J
of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
as
(\{\ast\},\|\mathchar 45\|_{J})
, where
\|{\ast}\|_{J}:=\{{\bf I}\}
.

•

A realizer for the left unitor
\lambda_{X}:J\widehat{\otimes}X\rightarrow X
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.{\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}p.{\bf e}p{\bf e})({\bf e}u)
.
A realizer for the inverse
\lambda_{X}^{-1}
is in
{\bf P}(\xi{\bf I})
.

•

A realizer for the right unitor
\rho_{X}:X\rightarrow X\widehat{\otimes}J
is in
{\lambda}^{\ast}p.{\bf P}p(\xi{\bf I})
.
A realizer for the inverse
\rho_{X}^{-1}
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.{\bf L}({\bf c}{\bf e})({\bf e}u)
.

•

A realizer for the associator
\alpha_{XYZ}:(X\widehat{\otimes}Y)\widehat{\otimes}Z\rightarrow X\widehat{\otimes}(Y\widehat{\otimes}Z)
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.{\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}v.{\bf L}M({\bf e}v))({\bf e}u)
, where
M\in{\lambda}^{\ast}pqr.{\bf P}p(r_{\xi}(r_{\xi}(\xi{\bf P})({\bf d}q)({\bf d}r)))
.
A realizer for
\alpha_{X}^{-1}
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.{\bf L}({\lambda}^{\ast}vw.{\bf L}(M^{\prime}v)({\bf e}w))({\bf e}u)
, where
M^{\prime}\in{\lambda}^{\ast}pq.{\bf P}(r_{\xi}(r_{\xi}(\xi{\bf P})({\bf d}p)({\bf d}q)))
.

•

The symmetry
\sigma_{XY}:X\widehat{\otimes}Y\rightarrow Y\widehat{\otimes}X
is the function sending
x\widehat{\otimes}y
to
y\widehat{\otimes}x
.
A realizer for
\sigma_{XY}
and
\sigma_{XY}^{-1}
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.{\bf L}({\bf c}{\bf P})({\bf e}u)
.

•

For objects
X
and
Y
, the exponential in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
is
Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X=(\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})}({\xi}_{\ast}X,Y),\|\mathchar 45\|)
, where
\|{f}\|:=\{r\in|{\mathcal{A}}|\mid\mbox{$r$ realizes $f$}\}
.

•

For maps
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
and
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
,
g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f
is the function sending a map
h:X\rightarrow Y
in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
to
g\circ({\xi}_{\ast}h)\circ d_{X}\circ({\xi}_{\ast}f)\circ d_{X^{\prime}}
, where
d_{X}:{\xi}_{\ast}X\rightarrow{\xi}_{\ast}{\xi}_{\ast}X
is the comultiplication of
{\xi}_{\ast}
.
A realizer for
g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f
is
{\lambda}^{\ast}uv.r_{g}(r_{\xi}u({\bf d}(r_{\xi}(\xi r_{f})({\bf d}v))))
.

•

The evaluation map
ev_{XY}:(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\widehat{\otimes}X\rightarrow Y
is the function sending
f\widehat{\otimes}x
to
f(x)
, that is realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.{\bf L}{\bf e}({\bf e}u)
.

•

For any map
f:Z\widehat{\otimes}X\rightarrow Y
in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
, there exists a unique map
g:Z\rightarrow Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X
in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
, which sends
z
to
x\mapsto f(z\widehat{\otimes}x)
.

g
is realized by
{\lambda}^{\ast}uv.r_{f}(r_{\xi}(r_{\xi}(\xi{\bf P})({\bf d}u))({\bf d}v))
.

•

Finally we show that the co-Kleisli functor
{\xi}_{\ast}:\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is strong monoidal.
We can take natural isomorphisms
{\xi}_{\ast}J\rightarrow I
and
{\xi}_{\ast}(X\widehat{\otimes}Y)\rightarrow{\xi}_{\ast}X\otimes{\xi}_{\ast}Y
in
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
as the identity functions.
Realizers for
{\xi}_{\ast}J\rightarrow I
and
{\xi}_{\ast}(X\widehat{\otimes}Y)\rightarrow{\xi}_{\ast}X\otimes{\xi}_{\ast}Y
are
{\bf e}
.
A realizer for
{\xi}_{\ast}J\rightarrow I
is in
{\lambda}^{\ast}u.u(\xi{\bf I})
.
A realizer for
{\xi}_{\ast}X\otimes{\xi}_{\ast}Y\rightarrow{\xi}_{\ast}(X\widehat{\otimes}Y)
is in
{\lambda}^{\ast}uv.r_{\xi}(r_{\xi}(\xi{\bf P})({\bf d}u))({\bf d}v)
.

The next proposition for categories of modest sets also can be shown in the same way as the above proposition.
Here since
X\widehat{\otimes}Y
in the above proof is not generally a modest set, we take the tensor product
\widehat{\boxtimes}
in
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
by the same way as Proposition
21
.
That is, we take
X\widehat{\boxtimes}Y=(|Z|,\|\mathchar 45\|_{Z})
by
|Z|:=(|X|\times|Y|)/\approx
, where
\approx
and
\|\mathchar 45\|_{Z}
are defined as the same ones in the proof of Proposition
5
.

Proposition 60
.

For an exch-rPLCA
({\mathcal{A}},\xi)
, the co-Kleisli category
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
is an SMCC and the co-Kleisli adjunction between is monoidal. ∎

Corollary 61
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{A}}
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra and
({\mathcal{A}},\xi)
is an exch-rPLCA.
Then we have a Lambek adjoint model as the co-Kleisli adjunction between the monoidal bi-closed category
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and the SMCC
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
(or between
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})_{{\xi}_{\ast}}
). ∎

Similar to exp-rPLCAs, adjoint pairs between
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras correspond to exch-rPLCAs.

Proposition 62
.

Let
(\delta\dashv\gamma):{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}
be an adjoint pair for a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
and a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{B}}
.

(1)

({\mathcal{A}},\delta\circ\gamma)
forms an exch-rPLCA.

(2)

({\delta}_{\ast}\dashv{\gamma}_{\ast}):\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{B}})
is a monoidal adjunction between the monoidal category
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and an SMCC
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{B}})
. If
{\mathcal{A}}
is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra, the adjunction is a Lambek adjoint model.

Proof 6.8
.

(1)

From Proposition
7
(
2
),
\delta\circ\gamma
is a comonadic applicative morphism.
We can take

{\bf c}
in
{\mathcal{A}}
as
{\lambda}^{\ast}xyz.{\bf L}x({\bf e}(M({\bf d}y)({\bf d}z)))
, where
M\in{\lambda}^{\ast}y.r_{\delta}(r_{\delta}(\delta N)y)
and

N\in{\lambda}^{\ast}yz.r_{\gamma}(r_{\gamma}(\gamma{\bf P})z)y
.

(2)

It follows from Proposition
23
.

Similar to exp-PLCAs, exch-PLCAs induce adjoint pairs between
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and
{\bf BCI}
-algebras.

Proposition 63
.

Let
({\mathcal{A}},\xi)
be an exch-PLCA.

(1)

We have a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\xi}=(|{\mathcal{A}}|,@)
with
x@y:=x(\xi y)
.

(2)

Let
\gamma:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{\xi}
be the identity function and
\delta:{\mathcal{A}}_{\xi}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
be the function
x\mapsto\xi x
. Then
\gamma
and
\delta
are applicative morphisms and
\delta\dashv\gamma
.

Proof 6.9
.

(1)

We have the
{\bf C}
-combinator in
{\mathcal{A}}_{\xi}
as
{\lambda}^{\ast}x.{\bf c}({\bf e}x)
.

(2)

Same as the proof of Proposition
58
(
2
).

For an example of exch-PLCA, we have the similar calculus to Example
6.1
.

{exa}

Suppose infinite supply of variables
x,y,z,\dots
.
Terms are defined grammatically as follows.

M::=x\;|\;MM^{\prime}\;|\;\lambda x.M\;|\;M\otimes M^{\prime}\;|\;\mbox{{\sf let} ${x\otimes x^{\prime}}$ {\sf be} ${M}$ {\sf in} ${M^{\prime}}$}\;|\;\xi M\;|\;\lambda^{\xi}x.M

Here
x
of
\lambda x.M
is the rightmost free variable of
M
, appears exactly once in
M
and is not in any scope of
\xi
.

x
of
\lambda^{\xi}x.M
need to appear exactly once in
M
.
Also we assume that for
let

{x\otimes x^{\prime}}

be

{M}

in

{M^{\prime}}
,
x^{\prime}
and
x
are the rightmost and the next rightmost free variables of
N
, appear exactly once in
N
and are not in any scope of
\xi
.

The rest is the same as Example
6.1
.

Finally we give an example of exch-PLCA based on
{\mathcal{T}}
of Example
3.2
.
This example is similar to the one introduced in
[
Tom22
]
.

{exa}

Let
T
and
|\mathchar 45|
be the same set and function defined in Example
3.2
.
First we give a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra
{\mathcal{T}}_{e}
from
T
.
Take
|{\mathcal{T}}_{e}|
as the powerset of
\{t\in T\mid|t|=e\}
,
and a binary operation
\circledcirc
on
|{\mathcal{T}}_{e}|
as
M\circledcirc N:=\{t_{2}\mid\exists t_{1}\in N,(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\in M\}
.
Then
{\mathcal{T}}_{e}=(|{\mathcal{T}}_{e}|,\circledcirc)
is a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra, where

•

{\bf B}=\{(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})\mid t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\in T\}
;

•

{\bf C}=\{(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{3}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})\mid|t_{1}|=|t_{2}|=|t_{3}|=e\}
;

•

{\bf I}=\{t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid t_{1}\in T\}
.

Take
\gamma:|{\mathcal{T}}|\rightarrow|{\mathcal{T}}_{e}|
as the function sending
M
to
\{t\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t\mid t\in M\}
and
\delta:|{\mathcal{T}}_{e}|\rightarrow|{\mathcal{T}}|
as the inclusion function.
Then these function forms an (functional) adjoint pair
(\delta\dashv\gamma):{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{e}
.
Here corresponding realizers are

•

\{((t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{2})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}))\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>((t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}))\mid t_{1},t_{2}\in T\}
realizing
\gamma
;

•

\{t_{1}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1}\mid t_{1}\in T\}
realizing
\delta
;

•

\{(t\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t\mid|t|=e\}
realizing
id\preceq\gamma\circ\delta
;

•

\{t\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(t\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t)\mid|t|\geq e\}
realizing
\delta\circ\gamma\preceq id
.

Remark 64
.

The above construction also can be applied to obtain exch-PLCAs on
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
of Example
3.3
and on
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime\prime}
of Example
3.4
.
While we gave exch-PLCAs by
T
, the same construction cannot be applied to obtain exp-PLCAs.
If we try to get some PCA of subsets of
T
, employing
M\circledcirc N:=\{t_{2}\mid\exists t_{1}\in N,(t_{2}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>t_{1})\in M\}
as the binary operation,
{\bf K}\circledcirc M\circledcirc N=M
hardly hold since the left hand side often lost information of
M
when
N
is nearly empty.

Remark 65
.

As we saw in Proposition
51
, exp-rPLCAs can be defined in the style using not the combinators
{\bf k}
and
{\bf w}
, but the applicative morphisms
[\mathbbold{\exm},\mathbbold{\exm}]
and
k_{i}
.
It is still unclear whether we can define exch-rPLCAs by the latter style, not using the combinator
{\bf c}
.
If we can characterize exch-rPLCAs by the latter style, we might construct exch-PLCAs using reflexive objects by the same way as exp-PLCAs and WPLCs (Definition
6.1
).

7.
Related work

This paper is an extended version of the earlier papers by the author
[
Tom21
,
Tom22
]
.
As a result of
[
Tom21
]
not introduced in this paper, we have “
{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebras” as a class of applicative structures.

{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebras are more general than
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras, and give rise to skew closed categories of assemblies (or modest sets).

Skew closed categories
, introduced in
[
Str13
]
, are categories with similar closed structures to closed categories, though some conditions needed in closed categories are not assumed.
(For instance, the natural transformation
i_{X}:(X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I)\rightarrow X
in a skew closed category is not necessarily invertible.)
Although skew closed categories and closed multicategories are generalizations of closed categories in different directions, from Proposition
42
, we can say that we cannot (canonically) obtain
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(or
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) that is a closed multicategory but not a skew closed category.
Details of these results are given in Appendix
A
.

Skew monoidal categories
introduced in
[
Szl12
]

are categories with the same components as monoidal categories but natural transformations (left and right unitors and associators) do not need to be invertible.
The relationship between skew monoidal categories and skew closed categories is similar to that between monoidal categories and closed categories.
Recalling the proof of Proposition
20
, we find that we use
{\bf I}^{\times}
only to realize
\rho_{X}^{-1}:X\otimes I\rightarrow X
.
The invertibility of
\rho_{X}
is not assumed in skew monoidal categories.
Thus, when we have
{\mathcal{A}}
as a “
{\bf B}{\bf I}{\bf L}{\bf P}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra,” we can show that
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is a skew monoidal category.

In
[
Has22
]
, the “extensionality” of combinatory algebras is investigated.
The extensionality defined in that paper is a more generalized condition than the standard one, seen in
e.g.
,
[
Bar84
]
.
By the extensionality in
[
Has22
]
, we can deal with polynomials and combinatory completeness for combinatory algebras that cannot be stated in the same way as Definition
2.2
and Proposition
1
, such as the braided case.
In our study, we do not need the discussions of the extensionality to state the combinatory completeness appearing in this paper, however, assuming the extensionality on an applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}
may cause some structures on
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
.
For instance, for an “extensional”
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
, since the
{\bf B}
-combinator always satisfies the axiom of
{\bf I}^{\times}
,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
become closed categories.
There are many other possible way to define classes of applicative structures than using the existence of certain combinators, and the extensionality is such one way.

The definition of bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras may look like “dual combinators” introduced in
[
DM97
]
.
Similar to bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras, in bianry operations of dual combinators, elements can act to elements from both left and right sides.
However, a dual combinatory logic has only one sort of application, whereas a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra has two sorts of applications.
Also the reductions of dual combinatory logic do not satisfy the confluence, while the confluence of the bi-planar lambda calculus holds.

In this paper, we referred several logics and their categorical models without recalling detailed definitions.
See
[
Gir87
]
about the linear logic.
And for the MILL and the categorical models that we deal with in this paper, see
[
See89
]
.
Also, for the Lambek calculus, the word “the Lambek calculus” has various means as logics, and we use this word to mean a variant of non-symmetric MILL with left and right implications in this paper.
Our treatment of the Lambek calculus and its categorical semantics are from
[
JIdP18
]
.
The basics about the Lambek calculus is in
[
Lam58
,
Lam69
,
MR12
]
.

In
[
Zei18
]
, the relationships between the planar lambda calculus and planar graphs are investigated.
In that paper, the bijection between rooted trivalent planar graphs and closed planar lambda terms is given, and it is shown that such graphs can be generated by combining a few kinds of “imploid moves.”
The theory corresponds to the combinatory completeness of
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and the planar lambda calculus.
Similarly, we can give the bijection between rooted trivalent planar graphs and closed bi-planar terms, but here the rooted trivalent planar graphs need to have two colored (“left” and “right”) vertexes.

8.
Conclusion

In section
2
and
3
, we introduced several classes of applicative structures and showed that they induce closed structures on categories of assemblies and categories of modest sets, as in Table
4
. (The results for
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras are newly presented in this paper.)
In section
4
, we showed that these classes are different ones by giving several examples.
In section
5
, we presented propositions that categorical structures of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
induce structures of
{\mathcal{A}}
, under some conditions.
(The propositions for
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebras are newly shown in this paper.)
By combining the results of the above, for instance we can say that we have
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
with a truly non-symmetric bi-closed structures, by using
{\mathcal{A}}
that is a bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra but not a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra.
In section
6
, we introduced exp-rPLCAs and exch-rPLCAs that give rise to categorical models for the linear exponential modality and the exchange modality on the non-symmetric MILL.
As an adjoint pair between a
{\bf BCI}
-algebra and a PCA induces an rLCA, an adjoint pair between
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and a PCA/
{\bf BCI}
-algebra induces an exp-rPLCA/exch-rPLCA.

Table 4.
Summary of the classes of applicative structures

Applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}

\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})

-algebras

Finally we give three issues for future work.
First, there are several unsolved problems we mentioned in this paper.
Those that we consider important are:

•

to show that the computational lambda calculus is not a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra (refer Section
4.3
);

•

to clarify conditions needed to show that
{\mathcal{A}}
is a PCA when
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(or
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) is a CCC (refer Remark
40
);

•

to clarify conditions needed to show that
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{\bf LP}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra/bi-
{\bf BDI}
-algebra when
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
is a monoidal closed category/monoidal bi-closed category (refer the end of Section
5
).

Second, most examples given in this paper are the standard ones like the term models.
We would like to find more interesting examples of applicative structures and adjoint pairs, that should be useful for investigating non-commutative logics and their models in a systematic way.

Third, for various categorical structures not given in this paper, we want to clarify what we need to construct them via categorical realizability.
For instance, we have said (in section
5
) that we cannot give
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(nor
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) that is a symmetric closed category but not an SMCC, in canonical ways.
Also we cannot give
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(nor
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) that is a closed multicategory but not a skew closed category.
As an example not yet mentioned, we cannot make
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
a braided monoidal category but not an SMCC.
Although there is a class of applicative structure,
{\bf B}{\bf C}^{\pm}{\bf I}
-algebras, nicely corresponding the structure of braided monoidal categories and the braided lambda calculus (investigated in
[
Has20
]
), the construction of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
cannot reflect the difference between two sorts of braids (realized by
{\bf C}^{+}
and
{\bf C}^{-}
) and turns braids into the symmetry.
To give the categorical structures listed above, we need to change the construction of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
), rather than trying to give conditions on applicative structures.
For instance, to make
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
a braided monoidal category (not an SMCC), we may need to change that the construction of
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
is based on
{\sf Sets}
, that is not only braided but also symmetric.

Appendix A
{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebras,
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and skew closed categories

Though classes of applicative structures appearing in this paper are subclasses of
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras, it does not conclude that realizability constructions for closed structures all require
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras.
Indeed, in
[
Tom21
]
, we introduced
{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebras, which is a more general class than
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras and gives rise to skew closed categories.

First we recall the definition of skew closed categories from
[
Str13
]
.

{defi}

A
(left) skew closed category

{\mathbb{C}}
consists of the following data:

(1)

a locally small category
{\mathbb{C}}
;

(2)

a functor
(-\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>-):{\mathbb{C}}^{op}\times{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
, called the internal hom functor;

(3)

an object
I
, called the unit object;

(4)

an natural transformation
i_{X}:(X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I)\rightarrow X
;

(5)

an extranatural transformation
j_{X}:I\rightarrow(X\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)
;

(6)

a transformation
L_{Y,Z}^{X}:(Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y)\rightarrow((Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X))
natural in
Y
and
Z
and extranatural in
X
,

such that the following axioms hold:

(1)

\forall X,Y\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
L_{Y,Y}^{X}\circ j_{Y}=j_{(Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}
;

(2)

\forall X,Y\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
i_{(Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}\circ(id_{(Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>j_{X})\circ L_{X,Y}^{X}=id_{(Y\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}
;

(3)

\forall X,Y,Z,W\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the following diagram commutes:
\textstyle{(W\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{Z,W}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{L_{Z,W}^{Y}}
\textstyle{(W\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{(Z\scalebox{0.6}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X),(W\scalebox{0.6}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}^{(Y\scalebox{0.6}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}X)}}
\textstyle{((W\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{Y,W}^{X}\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}id}
\textstyle{((W\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X))\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>((Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}L_{Y,Z}^{X}}
\textstyle{((W\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X))\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(Z\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>Y)}

(4)

\forall X,Y\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
(i_{Y}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>id_{(X\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}I)})\circ L_{X,Y}^{I}=id_{Y}\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>i_{X}
;

(5)

i_{I}\circ j_{I}=id_{I}
.

A skew closed category is called
left normal
when the function
\gamma:{\mathbb{C}}(X,Y)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}(I,Y\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>X)
sending
f:X\rightarrow Y
to

(f\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>id_{X})\circ j_{X}
is invertible for any
X,Y\in{\mathbb{C}}
.

There is a categorical structure called
skew monoidal categories
introduced in
[
Szl12
]
, which have the same components as monoidal categories but the invertibility of unitors and associators are not assumed.
Skew closed categories are the categorical structures determined from skew monoidal categories, like closed categories are determined from monoidal categories.
Obviously, closed categories are also left normal skew closed categories.

We investigated categorical realizability for skew closed categories in
[
Tom21
]
and next we recall some of the results.

{defi}

A total applicative structure
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebra
iff it contains
{\bf B}
,
{\bf I}
,
{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}
and
{a}^{\circ}
for each
a\in|{\mathcal{A}}|
is an element of
|{\mathcal{A}}|
such that
\forall x,y\in|{\mathcal{A}}|,({a}^{\circ})xy=x(ay)
.

Since
({\bf B}{a}^{\bullet}{\bf B})xy=x(ay)
, any
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra is also a
{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebra.
By the similar way to the proof of Proposition
33
, we can show the class of
{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebras is different from the class of
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras by using a freely constructed
{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebra (with constants).

Proposition 66
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebra,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
are left normal skew closed categories.

The proof is almost the same as Proposition
17
.
Here for maps
f
and
g
, we give a realizer of
(g\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>f)
as
{\bf B}{(r_{f})}^{\circ}({\bf B}r_{g})
.

It is still not clear whether
{\mathcal{A}}
need to be a
{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebra to make
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(or
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) a skew closed category, like propositions in Section
5
.
(In the similar setting to Proposition
43
and Proposition
49
, though we can show the existence of
{\bf B}
,
{\bf I}
and
{(\mathchar 45)}^{\circ}
, we cannot show there is
{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}
.)

Since
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras are
{\bf B}{\bf I}{{\bf I}}^{\bullet}(\mathchar 45)^{\circ}
-algebras, the next holds.

Corollary 67
.

When
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra,
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
and
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
are skew closed categories.

From Proposition
42
, we can say that we cannot (canonically) obtain
\sf{Asm}({\mathcal{A}})
(nor
\sf{Mod}({\mathcal{A}})
) that is a closed multicategory but not a skew closed category.
Although closed multicategories are a generalized closed categorical structure in a different direction from skew closed categories, skew closed categories are more general than closed multicategories as the categorical structures appearing in categories of assemblies.

Moreover, when constructing applicative structures from reflexive objects, skew closed categories can give even
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebras, as well as closed multicategories give.

{exa}

Suppose a skew closed category
{\mathbb{C}}
and an object
V
with a retraction

r:(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\triangleleft V:s
.
Then
{\mathbb{C}}(I,V)
forms a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.

•

For
M,N:I\rightarrow V
, the application is defined as

I\xrightarrow{M}V\xrightarrow{s}V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V\xrightarrow{id_{V}\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}N}V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I\xrightarrow{i_{V}}V.

•

The
{\bf B}
-combinator is

I\xrightarrow{j_{V\scalebox{0.6}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}V}}(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\xrightarrow{L_{V,V}^{V}\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}s}((V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V))\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V

\xrightarrow{(r\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}s)\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}id_{V}}V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V\xrightarrow{r\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}id_{V}}V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V\xrightarrow{r}V.

•

The
{\bf I}
-combinator is
r\circ j_{V}
.

•

Given arbitrary
M:I\rightarrow V
,
{M}^{\bullet}
is

I\xrightarrow{j_{V}}V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V\xrightarrow{s\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}id_{V}}(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V\xrightarrow{(id_{V}\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}M)\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}id_{V}}(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>I)\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V\xrightarrow{i_{V}\scalebox{0.75}{\reflectbox{$\multimap$}}id_{V}}V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V\xrightarrow{r}V.

{exa}

Suppose a closed multicategory
{\mathbb{C}}
and an object
V
with a retraction

r:\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(V;V)\triangleleft V:s
.
Then
{\mathbb{C}}(;V)
forms a
{\bf BI}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.

•

For
M,N\in\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(;V)
, the application is defined as

\xrightarrow{M,N}V,V\xrightarrow{s,id_{V}}\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(V;V),V\xrightarrow{ev}V.

•

Take a map
f:V,V,V\rightarrow V
as

V,V,V\xrightarrow{id_{V},s,id_{V}}V,\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(V;V),V\xrightarrow{s,ev}\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(V;V),V\xrightarrow{ev}V.

The
{\bf B}
-combinator is given as
r\circ\Lambda_{;V;V}(r\circ\Lambda_{V;V;V}(r\circ\Lambda_{V,V;V;V}(f)))
. Here
\Lambda
is the function in Definition
3.1
.

•

The
{\bf I}
-combinator is
r\circ\Lambda_{;V;V}(id_{V})
.

•

Given arbitrary
M\in\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(;V)
,
{M}^{\bullet}
is
r\circ\Lambda_{;V;V}(ev\circ(s,M))
.

When we assume the retraction
r:(V\>\reflectbox{$\multimap$}\>V)\rightarrow V
of Example
A
is an isomorphism, the
{\bf B}
-combinator further satisfies the axiom of
{\bf I}^{\times}
and
{\mathbb{C}}(I,V)
forms a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.
Similarly, when we assume the retraction
r:\underline{{\mathbb{C}}}(V;V)\rightarrow V
of Example
A
is an isomorphism, the
{\bf B}
-combinator satisfies the axiom of
{\bf I}^{\times}
and
{\mathbb{C}}(;V)
forms a
{\bf BI}{\bf I}^{\times}{(\mathchar 45)}^{\bullet}
-algebra.
